Properties and Characterisations of Cofree Cartesian Differential Categories
Jean-Simon Pacaud Lemay

Abstract
Cartesian differential categories come equipped with a differential operator which formalises the total derivative from multivariable calculus. Cofree Cartesian differential categories always exist over a specified base category, where the general construction is based on Faà di Bruno’s formula. A natural question to ask is, when given an arbitrary Cartesian differential category, how can one check if it is cofree without knowing the base category? In this paper, we provide characterisations of cofree Cartesian differential categories without specifying a base category. The key to these characterisations is, surprisingly, maps whose derivatives are zero, which we call differential constants. One characterisation is in terms of the homsets being complete ultrametric spaces, where the ultrametric is induced by differential constants, which is similar to the metric for power series. Another characterisation is as algebras of a monad. In either characterisation, the base category is the category of differential constants. We also discuss other basic properties of cofree Cartesian differential categories, such as the linear maps, and explain how many well-known Cartesian differential categories (such as polynomial or smooth functions) are not cofree.



Acknowledgements.

The author would also like to thank the organizers of the 22nd meeting of the Yorkshire and Midlands Category Theory Seminar (YAMCATS) for inviting me to give a talk on the subject of this research paper, and to the seminar’s audience for their questions and comments. In particular, the author would like to thank Steve Vickers for pointing out the metric was actually an
ultra
metric. For this research, the author was financially supported by a JSPS Postdoctoral Fellowship, Award #: P21746.

1
Introduction

Cartesian differential categories, introduced by Blute, Cockett, and Seely in
[
3
]
, are one of the cornerstones in the theory of differential categories, which, as the name suggests, uses category theory to provide and study the foundations and applications of differentiation in a variety of contexts. Briefly, a Cartesian differential category (Definition
2.5
) is a category with finite products such that each hom-set is a
k
-module, for some fixed commutative semiring
k
, which allows for zero maps and sums of maps (Definition
2.1
&
2.2
), and also comes equipped with a differential combinator
\mathsf{D}
, which for every map
{f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B}
produces its derivatives
\mathsf{D}[f]:A\times A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
. The differential combinator satisfies seven axioms, known as
[CD.1]
to
[CD.7]
, which formalise the basic identities of the total derivative from multivariable differential calculus such as the chain rule, linearity in vector argument, symmetry of partial derivatives, etc. Cartesian differential categories have been able to formalise various concepts of differential calculus such as solving differential equations and exponential functions
[
17
]
, de Rham cohomology
[
13
]
, tangent bundles
[
6
]
, and integration
[
8
]
. Cartesian differential categories have also found applications throughout computer science, specifically for categorical semantics differential
\lambda
-calculus
[
5
]
, and for the foundations of differentiable programming languages
[
12
]
, and automatic differentiation and machine learning
[
7
,
11
]
.

There are many interesting examples of Cartesian differential categories, with the two primary examples being based on differentiating polynomials and differentiating smooth functions (Example
2.7
.(
i
) & (
ii
)). The central notion of study in this paper are
cofree
Cartesian differential categories (Definition
3.1
) which satisfy a couniversal property over a base category amongst Cartesian differential categories. It is worth mentioning that Cartesian differential categories are essentially algebraic, and so it follows from standard results that free Cartesian differential categories exist: they can be given by the term logic for Cartesian differential categories
[
3
, Section 4]
. On the other hand, the fact that cofree Cartesian differential categories always exist does not follow any standard theory. This is an interesting similarity that the theory of differential categories shares with the theory of differential algebras, in the sense that cofree differential algebras also exist, called Hurwitz series rings
[
15
]
.

The original construction of cofree Cartesian differential categories is called the Faà di Bruno construction (Definition
3.7
), as first introduced by Cockett and Seely in
[
10
]
. The name comes from the fact that composition in this cofree Cartesian differential category is given by a generalisation of Faà di Bruno’s formula for higher order derivatives
[
14
, Lemma 3.4]
. Maps in the Faà di Bruno construction are called Faà di Bruno sequences (Definition
3.6
), which are sequences
(f^{0},f^{1},f^{2},\ldots)
of maps of the base category where the
n
-th is interpreted as the
n
-th higher order derivative of the
0
-th term (Definition
2.10
). The Faà di Bruno construction was then used by Garner and the author in
[
14
]
to prove two fundamental results for Cartesian differential categories. The first was characterising Cartesian differential categories as
skew-
enriched categories
[
14
, Theorem 6.4]
, while the second was proving that every Cartesian differential category embeds into the coKleisli category of a differential category
[
14
, Theorem 8.7]
.

Properties and applications of the Faà di Bruno construction have not yet been explored much. This is most likely due to the fact that composition and differentiation in the Faà di Bruno construction are somewhat complicated, making it somewhat challenging to work directly inside the Faà di Bruno construction. This is unsurprising since, famously, Faà di Bruno’s formula is also quite complex and combinatorial. To simplify working with the Faà di Bruno construction, Cockett and Seely used the term logic and trees in
[
10
]
, while Garner and the author used some pragmatic combinatorial notation
[
10
]
. However, in both cases, these techniques are pretty involved and heavy on notation. Another general construction of cofree Cartesian differential categories was then introduced by the author in
[
16
]
, in the hopes that this construction would be easier to work with. Unfortunately, this is still not quite the case. While differentiation is much simpler and composition is somewhat easier to work with, the trade-off is that maps in this alternative construction are much more complicated. Thus, working with these general constructions of cofree Cartesian differential categories is not as straightforward as one would hope.

The main objective of this paper is to demonstrate that one does not necessarily need to understand the internal workings of a cofree Cartesian differential category to study it. Indeed, simply from knowing that a Cartesian differential category is cofree over a base category, we may use its couniversal property to derive many interesting properties and structures. Of course, any cofree Cartesian differential category will be isomorphic to the Faà di Bruno construction of its base category (Lemma
3.11
), so these results may be derived by working with the Faà di Bruno construction directly. However, since working in the Faà di Bruno construction directly is somewhat tricky, the goal of this paper is to do as many proofs using only the couniversal property and avoid working directly with the Faà di Bruno construction (especially its composition and differentiation) as much as possible. In Section
4
, we show that in a cofree Cartesian differential category the differential linear maps (Definition
2.6
) correspond to the
k
-linear maps of the base category (Proposition
4.1
), while the
k
-linear maps instead correspond to pairs of
k
-linear maps in the base category (Proposition
4.3
). In Section
5
, we explain how every hom-set of a cofree Cartesian differential category is, in fact, a complete ultrametric space (Proposition
5.2
), where the metric is given by the functor to the base category and the higher order derivative, and is similar to the metric for power series or Hurwitz series
[
15
]
.

We also discuss how for certain base categories, a more concrete and familiar description of the cofree Cartesian differential category is possible. In particular, we provide a very simple description of the cofree Cartesian differential category over a category with finite biproducts (Proposition
3.18
). Another example we discuss, as first shown in
[
14
, Proposition 4.9]
, is that for the category of
k
-modules and arbitrary functions between them, the cofree Cartesian differential category over it is given by the coKleisli category of a comonad on the category of
k
-modules (Example
3.15
). In fact, this is an example of a monoidal differential category
[
2
, Definition 2.4]
, and in particular, a categorical model of differential linear logic studied by Clift and Murfet in
[
4
, Section 3]
. In the case where
k
is the real numbers or the complex numbers, the category of (finitely generated) cofree cocommutative
k
-coalgebras is a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category over the category of (finite-dimensional)
k
-vector spaces and arbitrary set functions between them (Example
3.16
).

Another main objective of this paper is to provide a base independent characterisation of cofree Cartesian differential categories. Indeed, a natural question to ask is, when given an arbitrary Cartesian differential category, how can one check if it is cofree without knowing the base category? To do so, we must first understand how to reconstruct the base category from a cofree Cartesian differential category. The answer is given by taking the maps whose derivatives are zero, which we call differential constants (Definition
6.1
). This is somewhat surprising since differential constants have, up till now, played no meaningful role in the theory of Cartesian differential categories, while differential linear maps are usually the more important maps. It is somewhat counter-intuitive that a Cartesian differential category is completely generated by maps whose derivatives are zero, yet this is the case for cofree Cartesian differential categories. However, in general, one cannot simply construct a subcategory of differential constants since, crucially, the identity map is not a differential constant. As such, in Section
6
, we introduce the notion of a differential constant unit (Definition
6.4
), which behaves like an identity map for differential constants, and if it exists, we can build the category of differential constants as desired. For a cofree Cartesian differential category, its category of differential constants is isomorphic to its base category (Proposition
6.8
). As a result, we then show that a Cartesian differential category is cofree if and only if it has a differential constant unit and is cofree over its category of differential constants (Theorem
6.13
). From this characterisation, we can easily check when a Cartesian differential category is not cofree by checking if it has a differential constant or not. As such, we can explain why the polynomial example or the smooth functions example are both not cofree (Example
6.11
).

To provide a completely internal and base independent characterisation of cofree Cartesian differential categories, we also need to ability to describe maps as converging infinite sums of their higher order derivative. From the point of view of the Faà di Bruno construction, we wish to able to say that a Faà di Bruno sequence is an infinite converging sum of the form
(f^{0},f^{1},\ldots)=(f^{0},0,\ldots)+(0,f^{1},0,\ldots)+\ldots
(Example
7.7
.(
i
)). In Section
7
, we explain that this can be done in a Cartesian differential category that has a differential constant unit which induces an ultrametric on the homsets, which we call being differential constant complete (Definition
7.1
), and such that the differential constants are well-behaved enough, which we call having convenient differential constants (Definition
7.3
). Therefore a Cartesian differential category is cofree if and only if it has a differential constant unit, is differential constant complete, and has convenient differential constants (Theorem
7.5
).

In Section
8
, we also provide a characterisation of cofree Cartesian categories as precisely the algebras of a monad on the category of Cartesian differential categories. This monad arises from the Faà di Bruno adjunction
[
14
, Corollary 3.13]
, which is the adjunction where the left adjoint is the forgetful functor and the right adjoint is given by the Faà di Bruno construction. The comonad of the Faà di Bruno adjunction was first studied by Cockett and Seely in
[
10
, Theorem 2.2.2]
, where they showed that the coalgebras of this comonad were precisely Cartesian
k
-differential categories
[
10
, Theorem 3.2.4]
. In more categorical terms, this says that Faà di Bruno adjunction is comonadic. For the induced monad of the Faà di Bruno, we show that a Cartesian differential category is an algebra of this monad if and only if it is cofree (Theorem
8.6
). From this, it follows that the Faà di Bruno adjunction is also monadic (Proposition
8.9
), and that the Faà di Bruno comonad is of effective descent type
[
1
, Section 3]
.

The characterisations introduced in this paper should simplify working with cofree Cartesian differential categories and hopefully lead to interesting new results and applications for cofree Cartesian differential categories. We conclude this introduction by discussing some ideas for future work to be done with cofree Cartesian differential categories. The first is, of course, considering and understanding the Cofree cartesian differential category over other interesting examples of base categories, such as over the category of polynomials or the category of smooth functions. We can also study differential equations and their solutions
[
17
, Section 5]
in a cofree Cartesian differential category or its de Rham cohomology
[
13
]
. We can also consider if it is possible to define integration in a cofree Cartesian differential category in the sense of
[
8
]
, or even differential exponential maps
[
17
, Section 3]
, which generalise the exponential function
e^{x}
in the setting of a Cartesian differential category. For a categorical model of the differential
\lambda
-calculus
[
5
]
, what does being cofree add to the story, or what are the applications of a differentiable programming language
[
12
]
that is cofree? Cartesian
reverse
differential categories were introduced in
[
7
]
to provide the foundations of reverse differentiation, which is used for automatic differentiation and machine learning, and, in particular, are also a Cartesian differential category with a transpose operator. So what are cofree Cartesian reverse differential categories, which should be cofree Cartesian differential categories with a transpose operator, and what practical applications can we use them for in machine learning
[
11
]
? Lastly, tangent categories
[
6
]
and Cartesian differential categories are closely related, where in particular, the subcategory of differential objects of a tangent category is a Cartesian differential category
[
6
, Proposition 4.7]
. Is there potentially a construction of cofree tangent categories, whose subcategory of differential objects is a cofree Cartesian differential category?

2
Cartesian Differential Categories

In this background section, we review the basics of Cartesian differential categories. For a more in-depth introduction to Cartesian differential categories, we refer the reader to
[
3
,
14
]
. In this paper, we will work with Cartesian differential categories relative to a fixed commutative semiring
k
, as was done in
[
14
]
, so in particular, we do not necessarily assume that we have negatives. When
k=\mathbb{N}
, the semiring of natural numbers, we obtain precisely Blute, Cockett, and Seely’s original definition and the theory from
[
3
]
.

The underlying structure of a Cartesian differential category is that of a Cartesian left
k
-linear category, which can be described as a category with finite products which is
skew
-enriched over the category of
k
-modules and
k
-linear maps between them
[
14
]
. Essentially, this means that each hom-set is a
k
-module, so in particular, we have zero maps and can take the sum of maps, but also allow for maps which do not preserve zeroes or sums. Maps which do preserve the module structure are called
k
-linear maps.

Definition 2.1

[
14
, Section 2.1]
A
left
k
-linear category
is a category
\mathbb{A}
such that each hom-set
\mathbb{A}(A,B)
is a
k
-module with scalar multiplication
\cdot:k\times\mathbb{A}(A,B)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{A}(A,B)
, addition
{+:\mathbb{A}(A,B)\times\mathbb{A}(A,B)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{A}(A,B)}
, and zero
0\in\mathbb{A}(A,B)
, and such that for any map
x:A^{\prime}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
,
{\_\circ x:\mathbb{A}(A,B)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{A}(A^{\prime},B)}
is a
k
-linear morphism, that is, for all
r,s\in k
and
f,g\in\mathbb{A}(A,B)
the following equality holds
1
1

1

In an arbitrary category, we use the classical notation for composition
\circ
as opposed to diagrammatic order which was used in other papers on Cartesian differential categories, such as in
[
3
,
16
]
for example. We denote identity maps as
{1_{A}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.99998pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A}
.
:

\displaystyle(r\cdot f+s\cdot g)\circ x=r\cdot(f\circ x)+s\cdot(g\circ x)

(1)

In a left
k
-linear category
\mathbb{A}
, a map
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
is said to be
k
-linear
if
f\circ\_:\mathbb{A}(A^{\prime},B)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{A}(A,B)
is a
k
-linear morphism, that is, for all
r,s\in k
and
x,y\in\mathbb{A}(A^{\prime},B)
the following equality holds:

\displaystyle f\circ(r\cdot x+s\cdot y)=r\cdot(f\circ x)+s\cdot(f\circ y)

(2)

For a left
k
-linear category
\mathbb{A}
, let
k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]
to be the subcategory of
k
-linear maps of
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{A}}:k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{A}
be the inclusion functor. A
k
-linear category
is a left
k
-linear category
\mathbb{A}
such that every map in
\mathbb{A}
is
k
-linear, so
k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]=\mathbb{A}
.

A list of basic properties of
k
-linear maps can be found in
[
3
, Proposition 1.1.2]
, such as the fact they are closed under composition and
k
-linear structure. Also, note that a
k
-linear category is precisely a category enriched over
k
-modules. It is worth mentioning that a category can be a left
k
-linear category in possibly different ways. So being a left
k
-linear category is a structure rather than a property.

We now add finite products to the story and require that the projections are
k
-linear. For a category with finite products, we denote the terminal object as
\ast
, the product by
A_{0}\times\ldots\times A_{n}
with projections
\pi_{j}:A_{0}\times\ldots\times A_{n}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A_{j}
, and denote the pairing operation by
\langle-,-,\ldots,-\rangle
.

Definition 2.2

[
14
, Section 2.1]
A
Cartesian left
k
-linear category
is a left
k
-linear category
\mathbb{A}
such that
\mathbb{A}
has finite products and all projection maps
\pi_{j}:A_{0}\times A_{1}\times\ldots\times A_{n}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A_{j}
are
k
-linear maps.

As mentioned above, Cartesian
\mathbb{N}
-linear categories are precisely Cartesian left additive categories
[
3
, Definition 1.2.1]
, and in this case, the
\mathbb{N}
-linear maps are precisely additive maps
[
3
, Definition 1.1.1]
. Conversely, every Cartesian left
k
-linear category is a Cartesian left additive category, and every
k
-linear map is an additive map. Properties of Cartesian
k
-linear categories can be found in
[
3
, Section 1.2]
, while a list of examples can be found in
[
14
, Example 2.3]
. Again, also note that while products are unique up to isomorphism, a category can possibly have multiple
k
-linear structures, which makes it into a Cartesian
k
-linear category. That said, we will only refer to Cartesian
k
-linear categories by their underlying category since, in this paper, there should be no confusion.

In a Cartesian left
k
-linear category, since not every map is
k
-linear or additive, the product
\times
is not a coproduct and, therefore, not a biproduct. That said, for any Cartesian left
k
-linear category
\mathbb{A}
, its subcategory of
k
-linear maps
k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]
is a
k
-linear category with finite biproducts. Conversely, a
k
-linear category with finite biproducts is precisely a Cartesian left
k
-linear category where every map is
k
-linear
[
14
, Example 2.3.(ii)]
.

A key concept for the story of this paper is the notion of functors between Cartesian left
k
-linear categories that preserve both the
k
-linear structure and finite products
strictly
. Of course, one could instead consider functors that preserve the products up to isomorphism. However, since this approach only adds a little to the story of characterising cofree Cartesian differential categories, we will do as in
[
14
,
16
]
and work with strict structure preserving functors. Furthermore, it follows that these functors also preserve
k
-linear maps.

Definition 2.3

[
16
, Section 3]
A
Cartesian
k
-linear functor
between Cartesian left
k
-linear categories
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{A}^{\prime}
is a functor
\mathcal{F}:\mathbb{A}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{A}^{\prime}
which preserves the product structure strictly, that is,
\mathcal{F}(A_{0}\times\ldots\times A_{n})=\mathcal{F}(A_{0})\times\ldots\times\mathcal{F}(A_{n})
and
\mathcal{F}(\pi_{j})=\pi_{j}
, and also preserves the
k
-linear structure strictly, that is,
\mathcal{F}(r\cdot f+s\cdot g)=r\cdot\mathcal{F}(f)+s\cdot\mathcal{F}(g)
for all parallel maps
f,g\in\mathbb{A}
and
r,s\in k
.

Lemma 2.4

[
3
, Lemma 1.3.2]
Let
\mathcal{F}:\mathbb{A}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{A}^{\prime}
be a Cartesian
k
-linear functor. Then if
f
is a
k
-linear map in
\mathbb{A}
,
\mathcal{F}(f)
is a
k
-linear map in
\mathbb{A}^{\prime}
. Therefore, there is a Cartesian
k
-linear functor
k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathcal{F}\right]:k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}^{\prime}\right]
, defined on objects and maps as
k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathcal{F}\right](-)=\mathcal{F}(-)
, and such that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathcal{F}\right]}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{A}}}
\textstyle{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}^{\prime}\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{A}^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}^{\prime}}

Observe that for any Cartesian left
k
-linear category
\mathbb{A}
, the inclusion functor
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{A}}:k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{A}
is trivially a Cartesian
k
-linear functor.

Cartesian differential categories are Cartesian
k
-linear categories that come equipped with a differential combinator, which is an operator that sends maps to their derivative. The axioms of a differential combinator are analogues of the basic properties of the total derivative from multivariable differential calculus. There are various equivalent ways of expressing the axioms of a Cartesian differential category. Here, we have chosen the one found in
[
14
, Section 2.2]
. Terminology-wise, in
[
14
]
we used the term Cartesian
k
-linear differential category, but for brevity, here we will use the term Cartesian
k
-differential category. It is also essential to notice that in this paper, unlike in
[
3
,
10
]
and other early works on Cartesian differential categories, we incorporate the convention used in the more recent works where the linear argument of the derivative is its second argument rather than its first argument.

Definition 2.5

[
14
, Section 2.2]
A
differential combinator

\mathsf{D}
on a Cartesian left
k
-linear category
\mathbb{X}
is a family of functions between the hom-sets:

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}:\mathbb{X}(X,Y)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{X}(X\times X,Y)

\displaystyle\frac{f:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y}{\mathsf{D}[f]:X\times X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y}

where
\mathsf{D}[f]
is called the
derivative
of
f
, and such that the following seven axioms hold:

[CD.1]

\mathsf{D}[r\cdot f+s\cdot g]=r\cdot\mathsf{D}[f]+s\cdot\mathsf{D}[g]
for all
r,s\in k

[CD.2]

\mathsf{D}[f]\circ\langle x,r\cdot y+s\cdot z\rangle=r\cdot\left(\mathsf{D}[f]\circ\langle x,y\rangle\right)+s\cdot\left(\mathsf{D}[f]\circ\langle x,z\rangle\right)
for all
r,s\in k
and suitable maps
x
,
y
, and
z

[CD.3]

\mathsf{D}[1_{X}]=\pi_{1}
and
\mathsf{D}[\pi_{j}]=\pi_{j}\circ\pi_{1}=\pi_{n+j+1}

[CD.4]

\mathsf{D}[\left\langle f_{0},\ldots,f_{n}\right\rangle]=\left\langle\mathsf{D}[f_{0}],\ldots,\mathsf{D}[f_{n}]\right\rangle

[CD.5]

\mathsf{D}[g\circ f]=\mathsf{D}[g]\circ\langle f\circ\pi_{0},\mathsf{D}[f]\rangle

[CD.6]

\mathsf{D}\left[\mathsf{D}[f]\right]\circ\left\langle x,y,0,z\right\rangle=\mathsf{D}[f]\circ\langle x,z\rangle
for all suitable maps
x
,
y
, and
z

[CD.7]

\mathsf{D}\left[\mathsf{D}[f]\right]\circ\left\langle x,y,z,0\right\rangle=\mathsf{D}\left[\mathsf{D}[f]\right]\circ\left\langle x,z,y,0\right\rangle
for all suitable maps
x
,
y
, and
z

A
Cartesian
k
-differential category
is a pair
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
consisting of a Cartesian left
k
-linear category
\mathbb{X}
and differential combinator
\mathsf{D}
on
\mathbb{X}
.

The convention of denoting Cartesian differential categories as pairs is not necessarily the norm in previous papers on the subject. We adopt this notation for two reasons: (1) to easily distinguish between Cartesian
k
-linear categories and Cartesian
k
-differential categories, and (2) later we will encounter cases of Cartesian
k
-linear categories that have more than one possible differential combinator. That said, we will often abuse notation and write
\mathsf{D}
for differential combinators. We only use different notations for differential combinators when there is a possibility of a Cartesian
k
-linear category having more than one differential combinator.

A discussion on the intuition for the differential combinator axioms can be found in
[
3
, Remark 2.1.3]
. Briefly, the axioms of a differential combinator are that:
[CD.1]
the differential combinator is a
k
-linear morphism,
[CD.2]
derivatives are
k
-linear in their second argument,
[CD.3]
what the derivative of identity maps and projections are,
[CD.4]
the derivative of a pairing is the pairing of the derivatives,
[CD.5]
the chain rule for the derivative of composition,
[CD.6]
derivatives are
differential
linear in their second argument, and lastly
[CD.7]
is the symmetry of the partial derivatives. Again, we mention that when
k=\mathbb{N}
, a Cartesian
\mathbb{N}
-differential category is precisely a Cartesian differential category in the original sense of Blute, Cockett, and Seely
[
3
, Definition 2.1.1]
, and conversely, every Cartesian
k
-differential category is a Cartesian differential category in the original sense. It is also worth mentioning that there is a sound and complete term logic for Cartesian differential categories
[
3
, Section 4]
. We will discuss examples of Cartesian differential categories below in Example
2.7
.

An important class of maps in a Cartesian differential category is the class of
differential
linear maps
[
3
, Definition 2.2.1]
. In this paper, we borrow the terminology from
[
14
]
and will instead call them
\mathsf{D}
-linear maps, to better distinguish them from
k
-linear maps.

Definition 2.6

[
14
, Definition 2.7]
In a Cartesian
k
-differential category
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
, a map
f:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
is
differential linear
, or simply
\mathsf{D}
-linear
, if
\mathsf{D}[f]=f\circ\pi_{1}
. Let
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]
to be the subcategory of
\mathsf{D}
-linear maps of
\mathbb{X}
and let
{\mathcal{J}_{(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})}:\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{X}}
the inclusion functor.

Properties of differential linear maps can be found in
[
9
, Lemma 2.6]
, such as the fact that they are closed under composition,
k
-linear structure, and product structure. In particular, every differential linear map is also
k
-linear
[
9
, Lemma 2.6.i]
, and as result, it follows that for Cartesian
k
-differential category
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
, the subcategory of
\mathsf{D}
-linear maps
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]
will be a
k
-linear category with finite biproducts
[
3
, Corllary 2.2.3]
. It is important to note that although
k
-linear maps and differential linear maps often coincide in many examples, in an arbitrary Cartesian differential category, not every
k
-linear map is necessarily differential linear.

Example 2.7

Here are some well-known main examples of Cartesian differential categories:

(i)

Let
k\text{-}\mathsf{POLY}
be the category whose objects are
n\in\mathbb{N}
and where a map
{P:n\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}m}
is a
m
-tuple of polynomials in
n
variables, that is,
P=\langle p_{1}(\vec{x}),\ldots,p_{m}(\vec{x})\rangle
with
p_{i}(\vec{x})\in k[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
. Then
k\text{-}\mathsf{POLY}
is a Cartesian
k
-differential category where the differential combinator is given by the standard differentiation of polynomials, that is, for
P=\langle p_{1}(\vec{x}),\ldots,p_{m}(\vec{x})\rangle:n\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}m
, its derivative
\mathsf{D}[P]:n\times n\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}m
is defined as the tuple of the sum of the partial derivatives of the polynomials
p_{i}(\vec{x})
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}[P](\vec{x},\vec{y}):=\left(\sum\limits^{n}_{i=1}\frac{\partial p_{1}(\vec{x})}{\partial x_{i}}y_{i},\ldots,\sum\limits^{n}_{i=1}\frac{\partial p_{n}(\vec{x})}{\partial x_{i}}y_{i}\right)

\displaystyle\sum\limits^{n}_{i=1}\frac{\partial p_{j}(\vec{x})}{\partial x_{i}}y_{i}\in k[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}]

A map
P:n\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}m
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear if and only if it is a tuple of monomials of degree 1, or in other words, if and only if it induces a
k
-linear map
k^{n}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}k^{m}
. Therefore,
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}[k\text{-}\mathsf{POLY}]=k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}[k\text{-}\mathsf{POLY}]
, and is equivalent to the category of finite-dimensional free
k
-modules and
k
-linear morphisms between them.

(ii)

Let
\mathbb{R}
be the set of real numbers. Define
\mathsf{SMOOTH}
as the category whose objects are the Euclidean spaces
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and whose maps are smooth functions between them.
\mathsf{SMOOTH}
is a Cartesian
\mathbb{R}
-differential category where the differential combinator is defined as the total derivative of a smooth function. For a smooth function
{F:\mathbb{R}^{n}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{R}^{m}}
, which is in fact an
m
-tuple
F=\langle f_{1},\ldots,f_{m}\rangle
of smooth functions
f_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{R}
, the derivative
{\mathsf{D}[F]:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{R}^{m}}
is defined as:

\mathsf{D}[F](\vec{x},\vec{y}):=\left\langle\sum\limits^{n}_{i=1}\frac{\partial f_{1}}{\partial x_{i}}(\vec{x})y_{i},\ldots,\sum\limits^{n}_{i=1}\frac{\partial f_{n}}{\partial x_{i}}(\vec{x})y_{i}\right\rangle

Note that
\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{POLY}
is a sub-Cartesian differential category of
\mathsf{SMOOTH}
. A smooth function
F:\mathbb{R}^{n}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{R}^{m}
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear if and only if it is
\mathbb{R}
-linear in the classical sense. Therefore,
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}[\mathsf{SMOOTH}]=\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}[\mathsf{SMOOTH}]
, and is equivalent to the category of finite-dimensional real vector spaces and
\mathbb{R}
-linear morphisms between them.

(iii)

Any
k
-linear category
\mathbb{B}
with finite biproduct is a Cartesian
k
-differential category where the differential combinator
\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{lin}}
is defined by precomposing with the second projection map:
\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{lin}}[f]=f\circ\pi_{1}
. Therefore, every map is
\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{lin}}
-linear by definition and so
\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{lin}}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}[\mathbb{B}]=\mathbb{B}
. As an explicit example, let
k\text{-}\mathsf{MOD}
be the category of
k
-modules and
k
-linear morphisms between them. Then
k\text{-}\mathsf{MOD}
is a Cartesian
k
-differential category where for a
k
-linear morphism
f:M\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}N
, its derivative
\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{lin}}[f]:M\times M\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}N
is defined as
\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{lin}}[f](m,n)=f(n)
. In fact, a Cartesian
k
-differential category in which every map is differential linear is a
k
-linear category with finite biproducts. That said, as we will see later, it is possible to equip certain
k
-linear categories with finite biproducts with other differential combinators
\mathsf{D}
such that not every map is
\mathsf{D}
-linear.

(iv)

An important source of examples of Cartesian differential categories, especially in computer science, are those which arise as the coKleisli category of a differential category
[
2
]
. We will discuss a particular example of this in Example
3.15
.

(v)

There are many other interesting examples of Cartesian differential categories in the literature. See
[
9
,
14
]
for lists of more examples of Cartesian differential categories.

We now turn our attention to functors between Cartesian differential categories, which are Cartesian
k
-linear functors that also commute with the differential combinator. It immediately follows that such functors also preserve differential linear maps.

Definition 2.8

[
16
, Section 4]
A
Cartesian
k
-differential functor

\mathcal{F}:(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathsf{D})
between Cartesian
k
-differential categories is a Cartesian
k
-linear functor
\mathcal{F}:\mathbb{X}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
such that
\mathcal{F}\left(\mathsf{D}[f]\right)=\mathsf{D}\left[\mathcal{F}(f)\right]
.

Lemma 2.9

Let
\mathcal{F}:(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathsf{D})
be a Cartesian
k
-differential functor. Then if
f
is a differential linear map in
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
,
\mathcal{F}(f)
is a differential linear map in
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathsf{D})
. Therefore, there is a Cartesian
k
-linear functor
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathcal{F}\right]:\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\right]
, defined on objects and maps as
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathcal{F}\right](-)=\mathcal{F}(-)
, and such that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathcal{F}\right]}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{J}_{(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{J}_{(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathsf{D})}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}^{\prime}}

OBserve that for any Cartesian
k
-differential category
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
, per Example
2.7
.(
iii
),
(\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{X}\right],\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{lin}})
is a Cartesian
k
-differential category and, moreover, the inclusion functor
{\mathcal{J}_{(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})}:(\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{X}\right],\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{lin}})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})}
is a Cartesian
k
-differential functor.

We conclude this section by discussing higher-order derivatives in a Cartesian differential category. There are two kinds: the total higher derivative and the partial higher derivative. The difference is that the former is the simply successive applications of the differential combinator, while the latter is successive partial derivations of the non-linear argument. Indeed, in a Cartesian
k
-differential category
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
, we can define the partial derivatives by inputting zeroes in the total derivatives. So for a map
f:X_{0}\times\ldots\times X_{n}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
, its
j
-th partial derivative

[
14
, Def 2.7]
in the argument
X_{j}
is the map
\mathsf{D}_{j}[f]:X_{0}\times\ldots\times X_{n}\times X_{j}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
defined as:

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{j}[f]=\mathsf{D}[f]\circ\langle\pi_{0},\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{n},0,\ldots,0,\pi_{n+1},0,\ldots,0\rangle

(3)

where
\pi_{n+1}
is in the
n+j
-th spot. We also say that a map
f:X_{0}\times\ldots\times X_{n}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear in its
j
-th argument

[
14
, def 2.6]
if
\mathsf{D}_{j}[f]=f\circ\langle\pi_{0},\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{j-1},\pi_{n+1},\pi_{j+1},\ldots\pi_{n}\rangle
. As mentioned above,
[CD.6]
is equivalent to the statement derivative are differential linear their second argument, that is,
\mathsf{D}_{1}\left[\mathsf{D}[f]\right]=\mathsf{D}[f]\circ\langle\pi_{0},\pi_{2}\rangle
.

Now for
n\in\mathbb{N}
, denote
X^{\times^{n}}
as a shorthand for the product of
n
-copies of
X
, with convention that
X^{\times^{0}}=\ast
and
X^{\times^{1}}=X
. Then for an arbitrary map
f:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
, applying the differential combinator
n
-times results in a map of type
\mathsf{D}^{n}[f]:X^{\times^{2^{n}}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
called the
n
-th total derivative. However, as explained in
[
14
, Sec 3.1]
, it follows from
[CD.6]
that there is a lot of redundant information in
\mathsf{D}^{n}[f]
, since many differential linear arguments. On the other hand, we can differentiate
f
once to get
\mathsf{D}[f]:X\times X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
, and then take the partial derivative in the non-differential linear argument to get a map of type
\mathsf{D}_{0}\left[\mathsf{D}[f]\right]:X\times X\times X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
, and so on and so… This will be the higher-order partial derivative of
f
.

Definition 2.10

[
14
, Definition 3.1]
In a Cartesian
k
-differential category
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
, for a map
f:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the
n
-th derivative
of
f
is the map
\partial^{n}[f]:X\times X^{\times^{n}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
defined as follows:

\displaystyle\partial^{n}[f]=\underbrace{\mathsf{D}_{0}[\mathsf{D}_{0}[\ldots\mathsf{D}_{0}}_{n\text{ times}}[f]\ldots]]

where by convention
\partial^{0}[f]=f
and
\partial^{1}[f]=\mathsf{D}[f]
.

Example 2.11

To highlight the difference between
\mathsf{D}^{n}
and
\partial^{n}
, let us work out a basic example. Consider the polynomial function
p(x)=x^{2}
. Then
\mathsf{D}
will differentiate all variables, while
\partial
will only differentiate the first variable:

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}^{0}[p](x)=x^{2}

\displaystyle\partial^{0}[p](x)=x^{2}

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}^{1}[p](x,y)=2xy

\displaystyle\partial^{1}[p](x,y)=2xy

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}^{2}[p](x,y,z,w)=2yz+2xw

\displaystyle\partial^{2}[p](x,y,z)=2yz

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}^{3}[p](x,y,z,w,a,b,c,d)=2zb+2yc+2wa+2xd

\displaystyle\partial^{3}[p](x,y,z,w)=0

In general, on polynomials,
\mathsf{D}
will never “stop”, while
\partial
will eventually hit zero.

The higher order total derivative
\mathsf{D}^{n}
and the higher order partial derivative
\partial^{n}
can actually be defined from one another
[
14
, Lemma 3.2]
. Indeed,
\mathsf{D}^{n}[f]
is, in fact, a sum of
\partial^{k}[f]
, for
1\leq k\leq n
, while
\partial^{n}[f]
can be defined by inputting zeroes in the appropriate arguments of
\mathsf{D}^{n}[f]
. While they are “interchangeable,” we will tell the story of this paper using the higher order partial derivatives
\partial^{n}
, for the reasons we will explain below.

Here are now some useful identities for higher-order partial derivatives:
[HD.1]
to
[HD.7]
are the higher-order versions of
[CD.1]
to
[CD.7]
, while
[HD.8]
tells us what the derivative of a higher-order partial derivative is. In particular,
[HD.5]
is Faà di Bruno’s Formula, which expresses the higher-order chain rule. To help write down Faà di Bruno’s Formula, let’s introduce some notation. For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
[0]=\emptyset
and let
[n+1]=[n+1]=\{1<\ldots<n+1\}
. Now for every subset
I=\{i_{1}<\ldots<i_{m}\}\subseteq[n+1]
, define
\pi_{I}:X^{\times^{n}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X^{\times^{m}}
to be
\pi_{I}:=\langle\pi_{i_{1}},\ldots,\pi_{i_{m}}\rangle
. We denote a
non-empty
partition of
[n+1]
as
[n+1]=A_{1}|\ldots|A_{k}
, and let
|A_{j}|
be the cardinality of
A_{j}
. Then Faà di Bruno’s Formula
[
14
, Lemma 3.14]
for the
n+1
-th derivative is given as a sum over the non-empty partitions of
[n+1]
.

Lemma 2.12

[
14
, Section 3]
In a Cartesian
k
-differential category
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
,

[HD.1]

\partial^{n}[r\cdot f+s\cdot g]=r\cdot\partial^{n}[f]+s\cdot\partial^{n}[g]
for all
r,s\in k

[HD.2]

For all
1\leq j\leq n
,
r,s\in k
and suitable maps
x_{0},x_{1}\ldots,x_{n}
and
x^{\prime}_{j}
:

\displaystyle\partial^{n}[f]\circ\langle x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,r\cdot x_{j}+s\cdot x^{\prime}_{j},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle

\displaystyle=r\cdot\left(\partial^{n}[f]\circ\langle x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{j},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle\right)+s\cdot\left(\partial^{n}[f]\circ\langle x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{j},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle\right)

[HD.3]

If
f
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear then
\partial^{0}[f]=f
,
\partial^{1}[f]=f\circ\pi_{1}
, and
\partial^{n+2}[f]=0

[HD.4]

\partial^{n}[\left\langle f_{0},\ldots,f_{n}\right\rangle]=\left\langle\partial^{n}[f_{0}],\ldots,\partial^{n}[f_{n}]\right\rangle

[HD.5]

The following equality holds:

\displaystyle\partial^{n}[g\circ f]=\sum\limits_{[n]=A_{1}|\ldots|A_{k}}\partial^{k}[g]\circ\left\langle f\circ\pi_{0},\partial^{|A_{1}|}[f]\circ\langle\pi_{0},\pi_{A_{1}}\rangle,\ldots,\partial^{|A_{k}|}[f]\circ\langle\pi_{0},\pi_{A_{k}}\rangle\right\rangle

[HD.6]

\partial^{n}[f]:X\times X^{\times^{n}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear in each of its last
n
-arguments.

[HD.7]

For every permutation
\sigma:\{1,\ldots,n\}\xrightarrow{\cong}\{1,\ldots,n\}
, and all suitable maps
x_{0},x_{1}\ldots,x_{n}
and
x^{\prime}_{j}
, the following equality holds:

\partial^{n}[f]\circ\langle x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle=\partial^{n}[f]\circ\langle x_{0},x_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,x_{\sigma(n)}\rangle

[HD.8]

The following equalities hold:

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}[\partial^{n}[f]]=\partial^{n}\left[\mathsf{D}[f]\right]\circ\left\langle\pi_{0},\pi_{n+1},\pi_{1},\pi_{n+2},\ldots,\pi_{n},\pi_{2n+1}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\partial^{n+1}[f]\circ\langle\pi_{0},\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{n},\pi_{n+1}\rangle+\sum\limits^{n}_{j=1}\partial^{n}[f]\circ\langle\pi_{0},\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{j-1},\pi_{n+j+1},\pi_{j+1},\ldots,\pi_{n}\rangle.

3
Cofree Cartesian Differential Categories

A Cartesian
k
-differential category is cofree if it satisfies a couniversal property amongst Cartesian
k
-differential categories over a base Cartesian left
k
-liner category.

Definition 3.1

A
cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category
over a Cartesian left
k
-linear category
\mathbb{A}
is a pair
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}\right)
consisting of a Cartesian
k
-differential category
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
and a Cartesian
k
-linear functor
\mathcal{E}:\mathbb{X}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{A}
which satisfies the following couniversal property: for any Cartesian
k
-differential category
(\mathbb{Y},\mathsf{D})
and Cartesian
k
-linear functor
{\mathcal{F}:\mathbb{Y}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{A}}
, there exists a unique Cartesian
k
-differential functor
\mathcal{F}^{\flat}:\mathbb{Y}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{X}
such that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\mathbb{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}}
\scriptstyle{\exists!~\mathcal{F}^{\flat}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}}

A Cartesian
k
-differential category
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
is said to be
cofree
if there exists a Cartesian left
k
-linear category
\mathbb{A}
and a Cartesian left
k
-linear functor
\mathcal{E}:\mathbb{X}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{A}
such that
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}\right)
is a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category over
\mathbb{A}
.

In
[
10
]
, Cockett and Seely provided a general construction of a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category over any Cartesian left
k
-linear category, which they called the
Faà di Bruno construction
. Therefore, we may state that:

Proposition 3.2

For every Cartesian left
k
-linear category, there exists a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category over it.

Before reviewing the Faà di Bruno construction, let us mention some basic yet important properties of cofree Cartesian differential categories. The following two lemmas are immediate consequences of the couniversal property, which are that: (1) the functor down to the base category is monic for Cartesian differential functors, and (2) cofree Cartesian differential categories can be restricted to full subcategories.

Lemma 3.3

Let
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}\right)
be a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category over a Cartesian left
k
-linear category
\mathbb{A}
. If
\mathbb{Y}
is a Cartesian
k
-differential category, then for any Cartesian
k
-differential functors
\mathcal{F}:\mathbb{Y}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{X}
and
\mathcal{G}:\mathbb{Y}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{X}
,
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{G}
if and only if the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\mathbb{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{G}}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}}

Lemma 3.4

Let
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}\right)
be a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category over a Cartesian left
k
-linear category
\mathbb{A}
. Let
\mathbb{B}
be a full sub-Cartesian
k
-linear category of
\mathbb{A}
, and let
\mathbb{X}|_{\mathbb{B}}
be the full sub-Cartesian
k
-differential category of
\mathbb{X}
whose objects are the objects
X
in
\mathbb{X}
such that
\mathcal{E}(X)
is in
\mathbb{B}
. Abusing notation slightly,
\left((\mathbb{X}|_{\mathbb{B}},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}\right)
is a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category over
\mathbb{B}
.

The following lemma explains that a cofree Cartesian differential category over a fixed base is unique up to isomorphism and that isomorphisms transfer being cofree.

Lemma 3.5

Let
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
and
(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathsf{D})
be Cartesian
k
-differential categories, and let
\mathbb{A}
be a Cartesian left
k
-linear category. Then:

(i)

If
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}\right)
and
\left((\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\right)
are both cofree Cartesian
k
-differential categories over
\mathbb{A}
, then there exists a unique Cartesian
k
-differential isomorphism
\mathcal{F}:(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathsf{D})
, so
\mathbb{X}\cong\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
, such that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}}
\scriptstyle{\exists!~\mathcal{F}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}}

(ii)

If
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}\right)
is a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category over
\mathbb{A}
and there exists a Cartesian
k
-differential isomorphism
\mathcal{F}:(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathsf{D})
, so
\mathbb{X}\cong\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
, then
\left((\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}\circ\mathcal{F}^{-1}\right)
is a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category over
\mathbb{A}
.

Proof:
For (i), define
\mathcal{F}:(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathsf{D})
using the couniversal property of
\left((\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\right)
and
\mathcal{F}^{-1}:(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathsf{D})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
using the couniversal property of
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}\right)
as respectively the unique Cartesian
k
-differential functors which make the following diagrams commute:

\textstyle{\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\exists!~\mathcal{F}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}}
\scriptstyle{\exists!~\mathcal{F}^{-1}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}}

Then, by Lemma
3.3
, since the diagrams on the left commute, the diagrams on the right commute:

Therefore

is a Cartesian differential isomorphism, and by definition it is the unique isomorphism such that the desired diagram commutes.

For
(ii)
, let
(\mathbb{Y},\mathsf{D})
be a Cartesian
k
-differential category and
{\mathcal{G}:\mathbb{Y}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{A}}
a Cartesian
k
-linear functor. Using the couniversal property of
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}\right)
, consider the induced unique Cartesian
k
-differential functor
\mathcal{G}^{\flat}:(\mathbb{Y},\mathsf{D})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
which makes the diagram on the left commute, which implies the diagram on the right commutes:

66.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 103.92784pt\raise 5.1525pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.1525pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 139.40405pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 139.40405pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern
