147.27415pt\raise-14.94957pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.77185pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}^{-1}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 147.27415pt\raise-24.58665pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-31.42415pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 69.93604pt\raise-31.42415pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 140.80614pt\raise-31.42415pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 147.27415pt\raise-46.08038pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.1525pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 147.27415pt\raise-55.42415pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-62.26164pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 69.93604pt\raise-62.26164pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 140.80614pt\raise-62.26164pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbb{A}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\end{array}

Since
\mathcal{G}^{\flat}
and
\mathcal{F}
are Cartesian
k
-differential functors, so is their composite
\mathcal{F}\circ\mathcal{G}^{\flat}:(\mathbb{Y},\mathsf{D})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathsf{D})
. Uniqueness follows from by Lemma
3.3
. Therefore
\left((\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}\circ\mathcal{F}^{-1}\right)
is a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category over
\mathbb{A}
.

\Box

Later in Lemma
6.12
, we will explain how the base categories are also unique up to isomorphism.

Let us now review Cockett and Seely’s Faà di Bruno construction, as first introduced in
[
10
]
. For a complete detailed account of the Faà di Bruno construction over Cartesian left
k
-linear categories, we refer the reader to
[
10
,
14
]
. It is also worth mentioning the slight differences in presentation in those references. In
[
10
]
, Cockett and Seely use the term calculus for Cartesian differential categories
[
3
, Section 4]
, while in
[
14
]
, Garner and the author use basic combinatorial and categorical notation.

The objects of the Faà di Bruno construction are the same as the objects of the base category. The maps in the Faà di Bruno construction consist of Faà di Bruno sequences, which are sequences that generalise sequences of the form
(f,\mathsf{D}[f],\partial^{2}[f],\partial^{3}[f],\ldots)
in a Cartesian
k
-differential category. The basic underlying property of a Faà di Bruno sequence is that the
n
-th map is multilinear and symmetric.

Definition 3.6

[
14
, Section 3.1]
In a Cartesian left
k
-linear category
\mathbb{A}
, a map
f:A_{0}\times A_{1}\times\ldots\times A_{n}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
is said to be:

(i)

k
-multilinear
in its last
n
-arguments if for all
1\leq j\leq n
,
r,s\in k
, and suitable maps
x_{0},x_{1}\ldots,x_{n}
and
x^{\prime}_{j}
, the following equality holds:

f\circ\langle x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,r\cdot x_{j}+s\cdot x^{\prime}_{j},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle=r\cdot\left(f\circ\langle x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{j},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle\right)+s\cdot\left(f\circ\langle x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{j},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle\right)

(ii)

symmetric
in its last
n
-arguments if for all permutations
\sigma:\{1,\ldots,n\}\xrightarrow{\cong}\{1,\ldots,n\}
, and all suitable maps
x_{0},x_{1}\ldots,x_{n}
and
x^{\prime}_{j}
, the following equality holds:

f\circ\langle x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle=f\circ\langle x_{0},x_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,x_{\sigma(n)}\rangle

For objects
A
and
B
in
\mathbb{A}
, an
\mathbb{A}
-Faà di Bruno sequence
from
A
to
B
, denoted
f^{\bullet}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
, is a sequence of maps in
\mathbb{A}
,
f^{\bullet}=(f^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}=(f^{0},f^{1},f^{2},\ldots)
where
f^{0}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
is an arbitrary map and
{f^{n+1}:A\times A^{\times^{n}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B}
is a map which is
k
-multilinear and symmetric in its last
n
-arguments. For
\mathbb{A}
-Faà di Bruno sequences
f^{\bullet}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
and
{g^{\bullet}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B}
, we say that
f^{\bullet}=g^{\bullet}
if for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
f^{n}=g^{n}
.

The rest of the Cartesian differential structure of the Faà di Bruno construction is given by generalising the properties of the higher-order partial derivatives from Lemma
2.12
. In particular, composition is given by Faà di Bruno’s formula
[HD.5]
, while the differential combinator is given by
[HD.8]
. The functor from the Faà di Bruno construction back down to the base category is defined as the identity on objects, while mapping a Faà di Bruno sequence to its
0
-th term.

Definition 3.7

[
14
, Definition 3.6]
For a Cartesian left
k
-linear category
\mathbb{A}
, the
Faà di Bruno construction
over
\mathbb{A}
is the Cartesian
k
-differential category
(\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{A}],\mathsf{D})
where:

(i)

The objects of
\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{A}]
are the same as the objects of
\mathbb{A}
;

(ii)

A map from
A
to
B
in
\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{A}]
is an
\mathbb{A}
-Faà di Bruno sequences
f^{\bullet}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
;

(iii)

Composition of
\mathbb{A}
-Faà di Bruno sequences
f^{\bullet}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
and
g^{\bullet}:B\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}C
is the
\mathbb{A}
-Faà di Bruno
f^{\bullet}\circ g^{\bullet}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}C
defined as:

\displaystyle f^{\bullet}\circ g^{\bullet}=\left(\sum\limits_{[n]=A_{1}|\ldots|A_{k}}g^{k}\circ\left\langle f\circ\pi_{0},f^{|A_{1}|}\circ\langle\pi_{0},\pi_{A_{1}}\rangle,\ldots,f^{|A_{k}|}\circ\langle\pi_{0},\pi_{A_{k}}\rangle\right\rangle\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

(iv)

The identity maps are the
\mathbb{A}
-Faà di Bruno sequences
1^{\bullet}_{A}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
defined as
1^{\bullet}_{A}=(1_{A},\pi_{1},0,0,\ldots)
;

(v)

The terminal object and the product of objects are the same as in
\mathbb{A}
;

(vi)

The projections are the
\mathbb{A}
-Faà di Bruno sequences
\pi_{j}^{\bullet}:A_{0}\times\ldots\times A_{n}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A_{j}
defined as
\pi_{j}^{\bullet}=(\pi_{j},\pi_{n+j+1},0,0,\ldots)
, while the pairing of
\mathbb{A}
-Faà di Bruno sequences is defined pointwise,
\langle f^{\bullet}_{0},\ldots,f^{\bullet}_{m}\rangle=\left(\langle f^{n}_{0},\ldots,f^{n}_{m}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
;

(vii)

The
k
-module structure of
\mathbb{A}
-Faà di Bruno sequences is defined pointwise, so addition is
f^{\bullet}+g^{\bullet}=\left(f^{n}+g^{n}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, scalar multiplication is
r\cdot f^{\bullet}=(r\cdot f^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, and zero is
0^{\bullet}=(0,0,\ldots)
;

(viii)

The differential combinator
\mathsf{D}
sends an
\mathbb{A}
-Faà di Bruno sequence
f^{\bullet}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
to the
\mathbb{A}
-Faà di Bruno sequence
\mathsf{D}[f^{\bullet}]:A\times A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
defined as follows:

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}[f^{\bullet}]=\left(f^{n+1}\circ\langle\pi_{0},\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{n},\pi_{n+1}\rangle+\sum\limits^{n}_{j=1}f^{n}\circ\langle\pi_{0},\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{j-1},\pi_{n+j+1},\pi_{j+1},\ldots,\pi_{n}\rangle\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

Let
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{A}}
be the functor defined on objects as
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{A}}(A)=A
and on maps as
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{A}}(f^{\bullet})=f^{0}
.

For the couniversal property of the Faà di Bruno construction
[
14
, Section 3.4]
, in order to construct the necessary unique Cartesian
k
-differential functors, we require the following two results: (1) that the higher order partial derivatives do indeed form a Faà di Bruno sequence (which is
[HD.2]
and
[HD.7]
), and (2) that Cartesian
k
-linear functors preserve Faà di Bruno sequences.

Lemma 3.8

[
14
, Lemma 3.2.(i)]
In a Cartesian
k
-differential category
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}\right)
, for any map
f:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
, the sequence
\partial^{\bullet}[f]:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
, defined as
\partial^{\bullet}[f]=(\partial^{n}[f])_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, is an
\mathbb{X}
-Faà di Bruno sequence.

Lemma 3.9

[
14
, Section 4]
Let
\mathcal{F}:\mathbb{A}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{A}^{\prime}
be a Cartesian
k
-linear functor. If a map
f:A_{0}\times A_{1}\times\ldots\times A_{n}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
in
\mathbb{A}
is
k
-multilinear and/or symmetric in its last
n
-arguments, then
\mathcal{F}(f):\mathcal{F}(A_{0})\times\mathcal{F}(A_{1})\times\ldots\times\mathcal{F}(A_{n})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathcal{F}(B)
is
k
-multilinear and/or symmetric in its last
n
-arguments. Therefore, if
f^{\bullet}:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
is an
\mathbb{A}
-Faà di Bruno sequence, the sequence
\mathcal{F}(f^{\bullet}):\mathcal{F}(A)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathcal{F}(B)
, defined as
\mathcal{F}(f^{\bullet})=(\mathcal{F}(f^{n}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, is a
\mathbb{A}^{\prime}
-Faà di Bruno sequence.

We may now state the couniversal property of the Faà di Bruno construction, where note that the functoriality of the induced Cartesian
k
-differential functor follows from Faà di Bruno’s formula for the higher-order chain rule. We also note that the
0
-th term of the
n
-th derivative of a Faà di Bruno sequence is the
n
-term of the sequence.

Proposition 3.10

[
14
, Theorem 3.12]
For any Cartesian left
k
-linear category
\mathbb{A}
,
\left((\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{A}],\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{A}}\right)
is a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category over
\mathbb{A}
, where in particular for any Cartesian
k
-differential category
(\mathbb{Y},\mathsf{D})
and Cartesian
k
-linear functor
{\mathcal{F}:\mathbb{Y}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{A}}
, the unique Cartesian
k
-differential functor
\mathcal{F}^{\flat}:(\mathbb{Y},\mathsf{D})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{A}],\mathsf{D})
such that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\mathbb{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}}
\scriptstyle{\exists!~\mathcal{F}^{\flat}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{A}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{A}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}}

is defined on objects as
\mathcal{F}^{\flat}(Y)=\mathcal{F}(Y)
and on maps as
\mathcal{F}^{\flat}(f)=\mathcal{F}(\partial^{\bullet}[f])
. Furthermore, for every
\mathbb{A}
-Faà di Bruno sequence
f^{\bullet}
,
\mathcal{E}(\partial^{n}[f^{\bullet}])=\partial^{n}[f^{\bullet}]^{0}=f^{n}
.

Since cofree Cartesian
k
-differential categories are unique up to isomorphism, a Cartesian
k
-differential category is cofree if and only if it is isomorphic to a Faà di Bruno construction. This provides our first characterisation of cofree Cartesian differential categories.

Lemma 3.11

Let
\mathbb{A}
be a Cartesian left
k
-linear category,
\mathbb{X}
a Cartesian
k
-differential category, and
\mathcal{E}:\mathbb{X}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{A}
a Cartesian
k
-linear functor. Then
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}\right)
a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category over
\mathbb{A}
if and only if the unique Cartesian
k
-differential functor
\mathcal{E}^{\flat}:(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{A}],\mathsf{D})
which makes the following diagram commute:

\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}}
\scriptstyle{\exists!~\mathcal{E}^{\flat}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{A}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{A}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}}

is an isomorphism.

Proof:
The
\Rightarrow
direction is a case of Lemma
3.5
.
(i)
. For the
\Leftarrow
direction, by Lemma
3.5
.
(ii)
, we would have that
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{A}}\circ\mathcal{E}^{\flat}\right)
is a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category over
\mathbb{A}
. But by definition,
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{A}}\circ\mathcal{E}^{\flat}=\mathcal{E}
. So
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}\right)
is indeed a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category over
\mathbb{A}
.

\Box

We may also restate Lemma
3.11
without specifying the base category.

Proposition 3.12

A Cartesian
k
-differential category
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
is cofree if and only if there exists a Cartesian left
k
-linear category
\mathbb{A}
for which there exists a Cartesian
k
-differential isomorphism
(\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{A}],\mathsf{D})\cong(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
.

An immediate consequence of this characterisation is that cofree Cartesian differential categories have essentially the same objects as their base category.

Corollary 3.13

Let
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}\right)
be a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category over a Cartesian left
k
-linear category
\mathbb{A}
. Then
\mathcal{E}
is bijective on objects. So in particular, for every object
A
in
\mathbb{A}
, there exists a unique object
X
in
\mathbb{X}
such that
\mathcal{E}(X)=A
, and if
\mathcal{E}(X)=\mathcal{E}(Y)
then
X=Y
.

Proof:
By Lemma
3.11
,
\mathcal{E}^{\flat}:\mathbb{X}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{A}]
is an isomorphism. But on objects,
\mathcal{E}^{\flat}(X)=\mathcal{E}(X)
. So since
\mathcal{E}^{\flat}
is bijective on objects,
\mathcal{E}
is also bijective on objects.

\Box

There is another general construction of cofree Cartesian differential categories given in
[
16
]
. In this construction, the maps are instead given by
\mathcal{D}
-sequences

[
16
, Defintion 4.2]
, which instead generalise sequences of the form
(f,\mathsf{D}[f],\mathsf{D}^{2}[f],\ldots)
. As discussed in
[
14
, Section 3.4]
, there is a bijective correspondence between
\mathcal{D}
-sequences and Faà di Bruno sequences, given precisely by the same formulas relating
\mathsf{D}^{n}
and
\partial^{n}
. The advantage of Faà di Bruno sequences is that they are simpler since there are no connections between the maps of the sequence, and they often provide a clearer story to understand. The disadvantage of Faà di Bruno sequences is that they are sometimes difficult to work with since composition and the differential combinator are given by somewhat complex formulas. On the other hand, the advantage of
\mathcal{D}
-sequences is that they are easier to work with since composition is given by a simpler formula and the differential combinator is defined by shifting the sequence to the left. The disadvantage of
\mathcal{D}
-sequences is that the components of the sequence are related to one another, and it is more difficult to check if something is a
\mathcal{D}
-sequence. Since, in this paper, we will not need to work with the composition or differentiation of Faà di Bruno sequences directly, we have elected to work with them instead since they provide a simpler story to tell. That said, we record the fact that cofree Cartesian differential categories can also be characterised as being isomorphic to the
\mathcal{D}
-sequences construction.

Proposition 3.14

A Cartesian
k
-differential category
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
is cofree if and only if there exists a Cartesian left
k
-linear category
\mathbb{A}
for which there exists a Cartesian
k
-differential isomorphism
(\mathcal{D}[\mathbb{A}],\mathsf{D})\cong(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
, where
(\mathcal{D}[\mathbb{A}],\mathsf{D})
is the
\mathcal{D}
-sequences Cartesian
k
-differential category defined in
[
16
, Definition 4.7]
.

Now that we have reviewed the general constructions of cofree Cartesian
k
-differential categories, let us consider particular examples where the cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category can be described more concretely. The first is an example that was discussed in
[
14
, Section 4.3]
, which provides a connection between cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category and differential linear logic. The second is a novel result which describes the cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category for
k
-linear categories with finite biproducts.

Example 3.15

Let
k\text{-}\mathsf{MOD}^{\omega}
be the category whose objects are
k
-modules and whose maps are arbitrary set functions between them. Then
k\text{-}\mathsf{MOD}^{\omega}
is a Cartesian left
k
-linear category
[
14
, Example 2.3.(i)]
where the finite product structure is given by the standard product of
k
-modules (which is not a biproduct in
k\text{-}\mathsf{MOD}^{\omega}
), and where the
k
-linear structure is given pointwise.
Furthermore, the
k
-linear maps in
k\text{-}\mathsf{MOD}^{\omega}
are precisely the
k
-linear morphisms, so
k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}(k\text{-}\mathsf{MOD}^{\omega})=k\text{-}\mathsf{MOD}
. It turns out that the cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category over
k\text{-}\mathsf{MOD}^{\omega}
can be described as the coKleisli category of a certain comonad
\mathcal{Q}
on
k\text{-}\mathsf{MOD}
which is defined in
[
14
, Definition 4.8]
. For a
k
-module
M
, let
\mathcal{S}(M)
be the free symmetric
k
-algebra over
M
,
\mathcal{S}(M)=\bigoplus\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{S}_{n}(M)
, where
\mathcal{S}_{n}(M)
is the
n
-th symmetric power of
M
. Then define
\mathcal{Q}(M)
as the coproduct indexed by the elements of
M
of copies of
\mathcal{S}(M)
:

\mathcal{Q}(M):=\bigoplus\limits_{x\in M}\mathcal{S}(M)

Following
[
4
, Definition 2.14]
, for
x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in M
, we denote
|x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle_{x_{0}}
to represent the pure symmetrized tensor
x_{1}\otimes^{s}\ldots\otimes^{s}x_{n}\in\mathcal{S}_{n}(M)
in the
x_{0}
-component copy of
\mathcal{S}(M)
in
\mathcal{Q}(M)
, and
|1\rangle_{x_{0}}
for the multiplicative unit of
\mathcal{S}(M)
in the
x_{0}
-component. Furthermore,
\mathcal{Q}
is in fact a
differential modality

[
14
, Section 4.2]
and therefore comes equipped with a deriving transformation
\mathsf{d}_{M}:\mathcal{Q}(M)\otimes_{k}M\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathcal{Q}(M)
, whose axioms are analogues of the basic algebraic properties of differentiation. This makes
k\text{-}\mathsf{MOD}
into a monoidal differential category
[
2
, Definition 2.4]
, and in particular a categorical model of differential linear logic
[
4
, Section 3]
. In general, the coKleisli category of a differential modality is a Cartesian
k
-differential category
[
3
, Proposition 3.2.1]
. Therefore, the coKleisli category
k\text{-}\mathsf{MOD}^{\mathcal{Q}}
is indeed a Cartesian
k
-differential category and also a categorical model of the differential
\lambda
-calculus. Recall that a map between
k
-modules
M
and
M^{\prime}
in
k\text{-}\mathsf{MOD}^{\mathcal{Q}}
is a
k
-linear morphism
f:\mathcal{Q}(M)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}M^{\prime}
. Then its derivative is the
k
-linear morphism
\mathsf{D}[f]:\mathcal{Q}(M\times M)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}M^{\prime}
defined as in
[
4
, Definition 5.11]
, which is worked out on pure symmetrized tensors to be:

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}[f]\left(|1\rangle_{(x_{0},y_{0})}\right)=f(|y_{0}\rangle_{x_{0}})

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}[f]\left(|(x_{1},y_{1}),\ldots,(x_{n},y_{n})\rangle_{(x_{0},y_{0})}\right)=f\left(|x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n},y_{0}\rangle_{x_{0}}\right)+\sum\limits^{n}_{j=1}f\left(|x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,y_{j},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle_{x_{0}}\right)

The functor
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Q}}:k\text{-}\mathsf{MOD}^{\mathcal{Q}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}k\text{-}\mathsf{MOD}^{\omega}
is the identity on objects,
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Q}}(M)=M
, and sends a
k
-linear morphism
f:\mathcal{Q}(M)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}M^{\prime}
to the function
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Q}}(f):M\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}M^{\prime}
defined as
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Q}}(f)(x)=f(|1\rangle_{x})
. As explained in
[
14
, Proposition 4.9]
,
\left((k\text{-}\mathsf{MOD}^{\mathcal{Q}},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}^{\mathcal{Q}}\right)
is a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category over
k\text{-}\mathsf{MOD}^{\omega}
. Using Lemma
3.4
, we can restrict this example to the finite-dimensional case and rewrite this example in terms of polynomials since
\mathcal{S}(k)=k[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
. It is also worth mentioning that for any Cartesian left
k
-linear category
\mathbb{A}
, its Faà di Bruno construction
\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{A}]
can be embedded into the coKleisli category of a generalisation of the comonad
\mathcal{Q}
on the category of
k\text{-}\mathsf{MOD}
valued presheaves of
\mathbb{A}

[
14
, Section 4.4]
. This result was then used to show that a Cartesian
k
-differential category embeds into the coKleisli category of the differential modality of a monoidal differential category
[
14
, Theorem 8.7]
.

Example 3.16

When
k
is an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero or
\mathbb{R}
,
\mathcal{Q}(V)
is the cofree cocommutative
k
-coalgebra over an
k
-vector space
V

[
4
, Proposition 2.11]
. In particular, this makes
\mathcal{Q}
a free exponential modality, and the resulting categorical models of differential linear logic and the differential
\lambda
-calculus were studied by Clift and Murfet in
[
4
, Section 3 & 5]
. In this case, the coKleisli category of
\mathcal{Q}
is equivalent to the category of cofree cocommutative
k
-coalgebras. Therefore, the category of (finitely generated) cofree cocommutative
k
-coalgebras is a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category over the category of (finite-dimensional)
k
-vector spaces and arbitrary set functions between them.

We now turn our attention to describing cofree Cartesian
k
-differential categories over
k
-linear categories with finite biproducts. To do so, we must first define a new construction of a Cartesian
k
-differential category over any Cartesian left
k
-linear category. We will also make use of this construction to help characterise the
k
-linear maps of a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category and in the proof of Proposition
6.8
. It is important to note that the differential combinator in the following definition does indeed copy the second map.

Lemma 3.17

Let
\mathbb{A}
be a Cartesian left
k
-linear category. Then
(\mathbb{A}^{\Delta},\mathsf{D}^{\Delta})
is a Cartesian
k
-differential category where:

(i)

The objects of
\mathbb{A}^{\Delta}
are the same as
\mathbb{A}
;

(ii)

The maps in
\mathbb{A}^{\Delta}
are pairs
(f,g):A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
where
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
is an arbitrary map and
g:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
is a
k
-linear map in
\mathbb{A}
, so
\mathbb{A}^{\Delta}(A,B)=\mathbb{A}(A,B)\times k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right](A,B)
;

(iii)

Composition is defined point-wise,
(f_{1},g_{1})\circ(f_{2},g_{2})=(f_{1}\circ f_{2},g_{1}\circ g_{2})
;

(iv)

The identity of
A
is the pair
(1_{A},1_{A})
.

(v)

The terminal object and the product of objects are the same as in
\mathbb{A}
;

(vi)

The projections are the pairs
(\pi_{j},\pi_{j}):A_{0}\times\ldots\times A_{n}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A_{j}
and where the pairing is defined point-wise,
\left\langle(f_{0},g_{0}),\ldots,(f_{n},g_{n})\right\rangle=\left(\langle f_{0},\ldots,f_{n}\rangle,\langle g_{0},\ldots,g_{n}\rangle\right)
;

(vii)

The
k
-module structure is defined point-wise, so the addition is
(f_{1},g_{1})+(f_{2},g_{2})=(f_{1}+f_{2},g_{1}+g_{2})
, the scalar multiplication is
r\cdot(f,g)=(r\cdot f,r\cdot g)
, and the zero is the pair
(0,0)
;

(viii)

The differential combinator
\mathsf{D}^{\Delta}
is defined as
\mathsf{D}^{\Delta}[(f,g)]=(g\circ\pi_{1},g\circ\pi_{1})
.

Furthermore, the functor
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{A}}:\mathbb{A}^{\Delta}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{A}
defined on objects as
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{A}}(A)=A
and on maps as
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{A}}(f,g)=f
, is a Cartesian
k
-linear functor.

Proof:
It is clear that
\mathbb{A}^{\Delta}
is indeed a Cartesian
k
-linear category, in fact, it is a sub-Cartesian
k
-linear category of
\mathbb{A}\times k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]
. For the differential combinator, since the second map is
k
-linear and the differential combinator involves the second projection, which gets rid of the first argument terms, it is straightforward to check that
\mathsf{D}^{\Delta}
satisfies the seven axioms
[CD.1]
to
[CD.7]
. So we leave this an exercise for the reader. Therefore, we conclude that
(\mathbb{A}^{\Delta},\mathsf{D}^{\Delta})
is indeed a Cartesian
k
-differential category.

\Box

If
\mathbb{B}
is a
k
-linear category with finite biproducts, recall that
k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{B}\right]=\mathbb{B}
. Therefore, maps in
\mathbb{B}^{\Delta}
are simply pairs of parallel maps in
\mathbb{B}
, so
\mathbb{B}^{\Delta}(A,B)=\mathbb{B}(A,B)\times\mathbb{B}(A,B)
. In particular, this means that
\mathbb{B}^{\Delta}
will also be a
k
-linear category with finite biproducts. As such, another way of proving that
(\mathbb{B}^{\Delta},\mathsf{D}^{\Delta})
is a Cartesian
k
-differential category is by using
[
3
, Corollary 2.3.2]
, which states that for a
k
-linear category with finite biproducts, to give a differential combinator is precisely to give Cartesian
k
-linear endofunctor which is the identity on objects and idempotent. For
\mathsf{D}^{\Delta}
, its associated functor is the one that sends maps
(f,g)
to
(g,g)
. Furthermore, it is important to note that the differential combinator
\mathsf{D}^{\Delta}
is different from the canonical differential combinator for biproducts
\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{lin}}
as defined in Example
2.7
.(
iii
). Indeed,
\mathsf{D}^{\Delta}[(f,g)]=(g\circ\pi_{1},g\circ\pi_{1})
, which gets rid of the first map, while
\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{lin}}[(f,g)]=(f\circ\pi_{1},g\circ\pi_{1})
, keeps the first map.

We will now prove that
(\mathbb{B}^{\Delta},\mathsf{D}^{\Delta})
is a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category over
\mathbb{B}
. This may be somewhat surprising since the maps in the Faà di Bruno construction were infinite sequences, while the maps in
\mathbb{B}^{\Delta}
are simply pairs. The reason for this collapse is that in any
k
-linear category with finite biproducts, the only
k
-multilinear maps in their last
n+2
arguments are zero maps. Therefore, a Faà di Bruno sequence must be of the form
(f^{0},f^{1},0,0,\ldots)
. In particular, the couniversal property, in this case, can be described using the induced
linearization combinator

\mathsf{L}

[
9
, Definition 3.1]
, which is an operator that makes any map differential linear. Indeed, in a Cartesian
k
-differential category
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
, for any map
f:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
, define
\mathsf{L}[f]:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}A
, called the linearization of
f
, as
\mathsf{L}[f]=\mathsf{D}[f]\circ\langle 0,1_{X}\rangle
. Then
\mathsf{L}[f]
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear, and furthermore,
f
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear if and only if
f=\mathsf{L}[f]

[
9
, Lemma 2.8]
.

Proposition 3.18

Let
\mathbb{B}
be
k
-linear category with finite biproducts. Then
\left((\mathbb{B}^{\Delta},\mathsf{D}^{\Delta}),\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{B}}\right)
is a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category over
\mathbb{B}
, where in particular for any Cartesian
k
-differential category
(\mathbb{Y},\mathsf{D})
and Cartesian
k
-linear functor
{\mathcal{F}:\mathbb{Y}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{B}}
, the unique Cartesian
k
-differential functor
\mathcal{F}^{\flat}:\mathbb{Y}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{B}^{\Delta}
such that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\mathbb{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}}
\scriptstyle{\exists!~\mathcal{F}^{\flat}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{B}^{\Delta}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{B}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{B}}

is defined on objects as
\mathcal{F}^{\flat}(Y)=\mathcal{F}(Y)
and on maps as
\mathcal{F}^{\flat}(f)=\left(\mathcal{F}(f),\mathcal{F}\left(\mathsf{L}[f]\right)\right)
.

Proof:
We must first prove that
\mathcal{F}^{\flat}
is a functor. Since identity maps are always differential linear by
[CD.3]
, we have that
\mathsf{L}[1]=1
. Therefore, it follows that
\mathcal{F}^{\flat}
preserves identities. To show that
\mathcal{F}^{\flat}
also preserves composition, first note that for all maps
f
of
\mathbb{Y}
, using
[CD.2]
and that all maps in
\mathbb{B}
are
k
-linear, we can compute that for any appropriate map
x,y
in
\mathbb{B}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(\mathsf{D}[f])\circ\langle x,y\rangle

\displaystyle=~\mathcal{F}(\mathsf{D}[f])\circ\left(\langle x,0\rangle+\langle 0,y\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=~\left(\mathcal{F}(\mathsf{D}[f])\circ\langle x,0\rangle\right)+\left(\mathcal{F}(\mathsf{D}[f])\circ\langle 0,y\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=~\left(\mathcal{F}(\mathsf{D}[f])\circ\langle\pi_{0},0\rangle\circ x\right)+\left(\mathcal{F}(\mathsf{D}[f])\circ\langle 0,y\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=~\left(\mathcal{F}\left(\mathsf{D}[f]\circ\langle\pi_{0},0\rangle\right)\circ x\right)+\left(\mathcal{F}(\mathsf{D}[f])\circ\langle 0,y\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=~\left(\mathcal{F}\left(0\right)\circ x\right)+\left(\mathcal{F}(\mathsf{D}[f])\circ\langle 0,y\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=~(0\circ x)+\left(\mathcal{F}(\mathsf{D}[f])\circ\langle 0,y\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=~0+\left(\mathcal{F}(\mathsf{D}[f])\circ\langle 0,y\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=~\mathcal{F}(\mathsf{D}[f])\circ\langle 0,y\rangle

So
\mathcal{F}(\mathsf{D}[f])
is independent of its first variable, so
\mathcal{F}^{\flat}(\mathsf{D}[f])\circ\langle x,y\rangle=\mathcal{F}^{\flat}(\mathsf{D}[f])\circ\langle 0,y\rangle
. Now using this and
[CD.5]
, we can compute that:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\flat}(g\circ f)

\displaystyle=~\left(\mathcal{F}(f),\mathcal{F}\left(\mathsf{L}[g\circ f]\right)\right)

\displaystyle=~\left(\mathcal{F}(f),\mathcal{F}\left(\mathsf{D}[g\circ f]\circ\langle 0,1\rangle\right)\right)

\displaystyle=~\left(\mathcal{F}(g\circ f),\mathcal{F}\left(\mathsf{D}[g\circ f]\right)\circ\langle 0,1\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=~\left(\mathcal{F}(g)\circ\mathcal{F}(f),\mathcal{F}\left(\mathsf{D}[g]\circ\langle f\circ\pi_{0},\mathsf{D}[f]\rangle\right)\circ\langle 0,1\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=~\left(\mathcal{F}(g)\circ\mathcal{F}(f),\mathcal{F}\left(\mathsf{D}[g]\right)\circ\langle\mathcal{F}\left(f\circ\pi_{0}\right),\mathcal{F}\left(\mathsf{D}[f]\right)\rangle\circ\langle 0,1\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=~\left(\mathcal{F}(g)\circ\mathcal{F}(f),\mathcal{F}\left(\mathsf{D}[g]\right)\circ\langle 0,\mathcal{F}\left(\mathsf{D}[f]\right)\rangle\circ\langle 0,1\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=~\left(\mathcal{F}(g)\circ\mathcal{F}(f),\mathcal{F}\left(\mathsf{D}[g]\right)\circ\langle 0,1\rangle\circ\mathcal{F}\left(\mathsf{D}[f]\right)\circ\langle 0,1\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=~\left(\mathcal{F}(g),\mathcal{F}\left(\mathsf{D}[g]\right)\circ\langle 0,1\rangle\right)\circ\left(\mathcal{F}(f),\mathcal{F}\left(\mathsf{D}[f]\right)\circ\langle 0,1\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=~\left(\mathcal{F}(g),\mathcal{F}\left(\mathsf{D}[g]\circ\langle 0,1\rangle\right)\right)\circ\left(\mathcal{F}(f),\mathcal{F}\left(\mathsf{D}[f]\circ\langle 0,1\right)\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=~\left(\mathcal{F}(g),\mathcal{F}\left(\mathsf{L}[g]\right)\right)\circ\left(\mathcal{F}(f),\mathcal{F}\left(\mathsf{L}[f]\right)\right)

\displaystyle=~\mathcal{F}^{\flat}(g)\circ\mathcal{F}^{\flat}(f)

So
\mathcal{F}^{\flat}
is indeed a functor. The linearizing combinator
\mathsf{L}
satisfies six axioms
[L.1]
to
[L.6]

[
9
, Definition 3.1]
which are analogues of the first six axioms
[CD.1]
to
[CD.6]
of the differential combinator. Since
\mathcal{F}
is a Cartesian
k
-linear functor and the linearizing combinator
\mathsf{L}
preserves the
k
-linear structure and projections, it follows that
\mathcal{F}^{\flat}
is also a Cartesian
k
-linear functor. To show that
\mathcal{F}^{\flat}
also preserves the differential combinator, first observe that by
[CD.6]
and
[CD.7]
it follows that the linearization of a derivative is
\mathsf{L}[\mathsf{D}[f]]=\mathsf{D}[f]\circ\langle 0,\pi_{1}\rangle
. Also, since
\mathcal{F}(\mathsf{D}[f])
is independent of its first variable, we have that
\mathcal{F}^{\flat}(\mathsf{D}[f])=\mathcal{F}^{\flat}(\mathsf{D}[f])\circ\langle 0,\pi_{1}\rangle
. So we compute:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\flat}\left(\mathsf{D}[f]\right)

\displaystyle=~\left(\mathcal{F}\left(\mathsf{D}[f]\right),\mathcal{F}\left(\mathsf{L}\left[\mathsf{D}[f]\right]\right)\right)

\displaystyle=~\left(\mathcal{F}\left(\mathsf{D}[f]\right)\circ\langle 0,\pi_{1}\rangle,\mathcal{F}\left(\mathsf{D}[f]\circ\langle 0,\pi_{1}\rangle\right)\right)

\displaystyle=~\left(\mathcal{F}\left(\mathsf{D}[f]\right)\circ\langle 0,1\rangle\circ\pi_{1},\mathcal{F}\left(\mathsf{D}[f]\right)\circ\langle 0,1\rangle\circ\pi_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=~\left(\mathcal{F}\left(\mathsf{D}[f]\circ\langle 0,1\rangle\right)\circ\pi_{1},\mathcal{F}\left(\mathsf{D}[f]\circ\langle 0,1\rangle\right)\circ\pi_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=~\left(\mathcal{F}\left(\mathsf{L}[f]\circ\right)\circ\pi_{1},\mathcal{F}\left(\mathsf{L}[f]\right)\circ\pi_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{D}^{\Delta}\left[\left(\mathcal{F}(f),\mathcal{F}\left(\mathsf{L}[f]\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=~\mathsf{D}\left[\mathcal{F}^{\flat}(f)\right]

So
\mathcal{F}^{\flat}
is a Cartesian
k
-differential functor. By definition, we also have that
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{B}}\circ\mathcal{F}^{\flat}=\mathcal{F}
, as desired. For uniqueness, suppose there was another Cartesian
k
-differential functor
\mathcal{G}
, such that
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{B}}\circ\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{F}
. This implies that on objects we have that
\mathcal{G}(Y)=\mathcal{F}^{\flat}(Y)
and on maps we have that
\mathcal{G}(f)=(\mathcal{F}(f),f^{\prime})
for some map
f^{\prime}
. Note we have that
f^{\prime}\circ\pi_{1}=\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{B}}(\mathsf{D}^{\Delta}[\mathcal{G}(f)])
. Since
\mathcal{G}
also preserves the differential combinator, we compute:

f^{\prime}=f^{\prime}\circ\pi_{1}\circ\langle 0,1\rangle=\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{B}}(\mathsf{D}^{\Delta}[\mathcal{G}(f)])\circ\langle 0,1\rangle=\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{B}}(\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{D}[f]))\circ\langle 0,1\rangle=\mathcal{F}\left(\mathsf{D}[f]\right)\circ\langle 0,1\rangle=\mathcal{F}\left(\mathsf{D}[f]\circ\langle 0,1\rangle\right)=\mathcal{F}\left(\mathsf{L}[f]\right)

Thus
\mathcal{G}(f)=\left(\mathcal{F}(f),\mathcal{F}\left(\mathsf{L}[f]\right)\right)=\mathcal{F}^{\flat}(f)
. So
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{F}^{\flat}
and therefore
\mathcal{F}^{\flat}
is unique. So we conclude that
\left((\mathbb{B}^{\Delta},\mathsf{D}^{\Delta}),\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{B}}\right)
is a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category over
\mathbb{B}
.

\Box

4
Linear Maps in Cofree Cartesian Differential Categories

In this section, we describe the
k
-linear maps and differential linear maps in a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category. We will show that the differential linear maps correspond precisely to the
k
-linear maps in the base category, while the
k
-linear maps instead correspond to pairs of
k
-linear maps in the base category. More precisely, we will show that the subcategory of differential linear maps is isomorphic to the subcategory of
k
-linear maps of the base category, while the subcategory of
k
-linear maps is isomorphic to the cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category of the subcategory of
k
-linear maps of the base category.

Proposition 4.1

Let
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}\right)
be a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category over a Cartesian left
k
-linear category
\mathbb{A}
. Then the functor
\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{lin}}:\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]
defined on objects and maps as
\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{lin}}(-)=\mathcal{E}(-)
, is a Cartesian
k
-linear ismorphism, so
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\cong k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]
, and it is the unique Cartesian
k
-linear isomorphism such that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\exists!~\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{lin}}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{J}_{(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})}}
\textstyle{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{A}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}}

In other words, for every
\mathsf{D}
-linear map
f
in
\mathbb{X}
,
\mathcal{E}(f)
is
k
-linear in
\mathbb{A}
, and conversely, for every
k
-linear map
f
in
\mathbb{A}
, there exists a unique
\mathsf{D}
-linear map
f^{\sharp}
in
\mathbb{X}
such that
\mathcal{E}(f^{\sharp})=f
.

Proof:
As explained in Section
2
,
k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]
and
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]
are
k
-linear categories with finite biproducts, and thus are also Cartesian left
k
-linear categories. Next, let us explain why
\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{lin}}
is well-defined. On objects, this is clear. On maps, recall that if
f
is
\mathsf{D}
-linear, then
f
is also
k
-linear, and therefore by Lemma
2.4
,
\mathcal{E}(f)
is
k
-linear in
\mathbb{A}
. Therefore,
\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{lin}}
is well-defined, and since
\mathcal{E}
is a Cartesian
k
-linear functor,
\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{lin}}
is also. Furthermore, by definition we also have that
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{A}}\circ\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{lin}}=\mathcal{E}\circ\mathcal{J}_{(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})}
, and since
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{A}}
is monic,
\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{lin}}
is the unique such functor. To define the inverse of
\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{lin}}
, we use the fact that
(k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right],\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{lin}})
is a Cartesian
k
-differential category, as defined in Example
2.7
.(
iii
). Therefore, by the couniversal property of
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}\right)
, there exists a unique Cartesian
k
-differential functor
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{A}}^{\flat}:(k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right],\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{lin}})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
which makes the following diagram commute:

\textstyle{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\exists!~\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{A}}^{\flat}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}}

Then define
\mathcal{E}^{-1}_{\mathsf{lin}}:k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]
on objects and maps as
\mathcal{E}^{-1}_{\mathsf{lin}}(-)=\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{A}}^{\flat}(-)
. On objects
\mathcal{E}^{-1}_{\mathsf{lin}}
is well-defined, while on maps, since every map
f
in
k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]
is
\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{lin}}
-linear by definition, by Lemma
2.9
,
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{A}}^{\flat}(f)
will also be
\mathsf{D}
-linear in
\mathbb{X}
. Therefore,
\mathcal{E}^{-1}_{\mathsf{lin}}
is well-defined, and since
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{A}}^{\flat}
is a Cartesian
k
-linear functor,
\mathcal{E}^{-1}_{\mathsf{lin}}
is as well. Since
\mathcal{E}\circ\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{A}}^{\flat}=\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{A}}
, it follows that
\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{lin}}\circ\mathcal{E}^{-1}_{\mathsf{lin}}=1_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]}
. For the other direction, recall that
(\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{X}\right],\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{lin}})
is also a Cartesian
k
-differential, and note that both
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{A}}^{\flat}\circ\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{lin}}
and
\mathcal{J}_{(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})}
are Cartesian
k
-differential functors of type
(\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{X}\right],\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{lin}})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
. Then, by Lemma
3.3
, since the diagram on the left commutes, the diagram on the right commutes:

3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 89.76816pt\raise-29.05309pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 40.4542pt\raise 5.6775pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.6275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{lin}}}$}}}\kern 96.23618pt\raise-15.93124pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.48434pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{A}}^{\flat}}$}}}\kern Then it follows from the diagram on the right that
\mathcal{E}^{-1}_{\mathsf{lin}}\circ\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{lin}}=1_{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]}
. So we conclude that
\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{lin}}
is a Cartesian
k
-linear isomorphism, and so
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\cong k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]
.

\Box

In order to prove the desired result for
k
-linear maps, we first need to discuss the derivative of
k
-linear maps. It turns out that the derivative of a
k
-linear map is also a
k
-linear map. Therefore, the subcategory of
k
-linear is a Cartesian
k
-differential category.

Lemma 4.2

[
3
, Propostion 2.3.1]
Let
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
be a Cartesian
k
-differential category. If
f
is a
k
-linear map in
\mathbb{X}
, then
\mathsf{D}[f]
is also a
k
-linear map in
\mathbb{X}
. Therefore,
(k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{X}\right],\mathsf{D})
is a Cartesian
k
-differential category and the inclusion functor
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{X}}:(k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{X}\right],\mathsf{D})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
is a Cartesian
k
-differential category.

Using Proposition
3.18
, we may show that
k
-linear maps in a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category correspond to pairs of
k
-linear maps in the base category.

Proposition 4.3

Let
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}\right)
be a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category over a Cartesian left
k
-linear category
\mathbb{A}
. Consider
(k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{X}\right],\mathsf{D})
to be a Cartesian
k
-differential category as defined in Lemma
4.2
and so consider the induced unique Cartesian
k
-differential functor
\mathcal{E}^{\flat}_{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}:(k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{X}\right],\mathsf{D})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]^{\Delta},\mathsf{D}^{\Delta})
that makes the following diagram commute:

\textstyle{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}_{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}}
\scriptstyle{\exists!~\mathcal{E}_{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}^{\flat}}
\textstyle{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]^{\Delta}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{P}_{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]}}
\textstyle{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]}

as defined in Proposition
3.18
, so on objects
\mathcal{E}^{\flat}_{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}(X)=\mathcal{E}(X)
and on maps
\mathcal{E}^{\flat}_{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}(f)=\left(\mathcal{E}(f),\mathcal{E}\left(\mathsf{L}[f]\right)\right)
. Then
\mathcal{E}_{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}
is a Cartesian
k
-differential isomorphism, so
k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\cong k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]^{\Delta}
, and it is the unique Cartesian
k
-differential isomorphism such that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\mathsf{k}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\exists!~\mathcal{E}^{\flat}_{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{X}}}
\textstyle{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]^{\Delta}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{P}_{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]}}
\textstyle{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{A}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}}

In other words, for every
k
-linear map
f
in
\mathbb{X}
,
\mathcal{E}(f)
and
\mathcal{E}\left(\mathsf{L}[f]\right)
are
k
-linear maps in
\mathbb{A}
, and conversely for every pair of parallel
k
-linear maps
f
and
g
in
\mathbb{A}
, there exists a unique
k
-linear map
(f,g)^{\sharp}
in
\mathbb{X}
such that
\mathcal{E}((f,g)^{\sharp})=f
and
\mathcal{E}\left(\mathsf{D}[(f,g)^{\sharp}]\right)=g\circ\pi_{1}
.

Proof:
First observe that by definition and Lemma
2.4
, it follows that
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{A}}\circ\mathcal{P}_{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]}\circ\mathcal{E}^{\flat}_{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}=\mathcal{E}\circ\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{X}}
as desired. Since
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{A}}
is monic and by Lemma
3.3
, we also have that
\mathcal{E}^{\flat}_{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}
is the unique such Cartesian
k
-differential functor. To construct the inverse of
\mathcal{E}^{\flat}_{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}
, consider the unique Cartesian
k
-differential functor
\mathcal{R}:(k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]^{\Delta},\mathsf{D}^{\Delta})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
such that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]^{\Delta}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{P}_{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]}}
\scriptstyle{\exists!~\mathcal{R}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}}
\textstyle{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}}

Now recall that every map in
k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]^{\Delta}
is
k
-linear, therefore
k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]^{\Delta}\right]=k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]^{\Delta}
. Then the restriction of
\mathcal{R}
to the subcategories of
k
-linear maps is of type
\mathcal{R}_{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}:k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]^{\Delta}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]
. We will now show that
\mathcal{R}_{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}
and
\mathcal{E}^{\flat}_{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}
are inverses of each other. First note that by definition of
\mathcal{R}
, we have that on objects
\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{R}(A))=A
and on maps
\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{R}(f,g))=f
. Since
\mathcal{R}
also commutes with the differential combinator, we compute that:

\mathcal{E}\left(\mathsf{D}\left[\mathcal{R}(f,g)\right]\right)=\mathcal{E}\left(\mathcal{R}\left(\mathsf{D}^{\Delta}\left[(f,g)\right]\right)\right)=\mathcal{E}\left(\mathcal{R}\left(g\circ\pi_{1},g\circ\pi_{1}\right)\right)=\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{R}(g,g))\circ\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{R}(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}))=g\circ\pi_{1}

So
\mathcal{E}\left(\mathsf{D}\left[\mathcal{R}(f,g)\right]\right)=g\circ\pi_{1}
and therefore it follows that
\mathcal{E}\left(\mathsf{L}\left[\mathcal{R}(f,g)\right]\right)=g
. Therefore, on object
\mathcal{E}^{\flat}_{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}\left(\mathcal{R}_{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}(A)\right)=A
, while on maps we compute:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\flat}_{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}\left(\mathcal{R}_{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}(f,g)\right)=\left(\mathcal{E}\left(\mathcal{R}(f,g)\right),\mathcal{E}\left(\mathsf{L}\left[\mathcal{R}(f,g)\right]\right)\right)=(f,g)

So
\mathcal{E}^{\flat}_{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}\circ\mathcal{R}_{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}=1_{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]^{\Delta}}
. For the other direction, note that
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{X}}
and
\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{E}^{\flat}_{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}
are Cartesian
k
-differential functors of type
(k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{X}\right],\mathsf{D})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
. Then, by Lemma
3.3
, since the diagram on the left commutes, the diagram on the right commutes:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[c]{c}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.31248pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-56.42665pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 87.62996pt\raise-68.41664pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.1525pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 179.94849pt\raise-63.26414pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 95.83234pt\raise-63.26414pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 179.94849pt\raise-63.26414pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbb{A}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\end{array}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{Lem.\ref{lem:cofree-lem1}}}}{{\Longrightarrow}}\begin{array}[c]{c}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.31248pt\hbox{\kern 17.71619pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-17.71619pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 46.66815pt\raise-23.39777pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.6275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{I}_{\mathbb{X}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 92.36432pt\raise-29.74855pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 39.65039pt\raise 5.6775pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.6275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{lin}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 77.71619pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 77.71619pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]^{\Delta}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 98.83234pt\raise-16.27582pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.1525pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{R}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 98.83234pt\raise-24.98915pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-31.82664pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 92.36432pt\raise-31.82664pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\end{array}

Then it follows that
\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{E}^{\flat}_{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}=1_{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]}
. So we conclude that
\mathcal{E}^{\flat}_{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}
is a Cartesian differential isomorphism and so
k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\cong k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]^{\Delta}
.

\Box

Remark 4.4

Proposition
4.3
also implies that the subcategory of
k
-linear maps of a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category is a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category over the subcategory of
k
-linear maps of the base category.

We have shown that differential linear maps correspond to
k
-linear maps in the base. But since every differential linear map is also a
k
-linear map, it also corresponds to a pair of
k
-linear maps in the base category. The following corollary explains that for differential linear maps, its associated pair is given by two copies of the same
k
-linear map in the base category.

Corollary 4.5

Let
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}\right)
be a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category over a Cartesian left
k
-linear category
\mathbb{A}
. Then the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{J}_{(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})}}_{\mathsf{lin}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}^{\flat}_{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1^{\flat}_{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]}}
\textstyle{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{lin}}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]^{\Delta}}

where
1^{\flat}_{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]}:k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]^{\Delta}
is the duplicate functor defined on objects as
1^{\flat}_{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]}(A)=A
and on maps as
1^{\flat}_{k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{A}\right]}(f)=(f,f)
, and where
{\mathcal{J}_{(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})}}_{\mathsf{lin}}:\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]
is the inclusion functor.

Proof:
Recall that if
f
is a
\mathsf{D}
-linear map in
\mathbb{X}
then
\mathsf{L}[f]=f
. Therefore, it follows that the diagram commutes.

\Box

Example 4.6

Here are the differential linear maps and
k
-linear maps in our examples of cofree Cartesian
k
-differential categories.

(i)

For a Cartesian left
k
-linear category
\mathbb{A}
, in its Faà di Bruno construction, the
k
-linear maps are the
\mathbb{A}
-Faà di Bruno sequences of the form
(f,g\circ\pi_{1},0,0,\ldots)
, for any parallel
k
-linear maps
f
and
g
in
\mathbb{A}
, while the differential linear maps are the
\mathbb{A}
-Faà di Bruno sequences of the form
(f,f\circ\pi_{1},0,0,\ldots)
, for any
k
-linear map
f
in
\mathbb{A}
.

(ii)

For a
k
-linear category
\mathbb{B}
with finite biproducts, in
\mathbb{B}^{\Delta}
, all maps
(f,g)
are
k
-linear maps, while the
\mathsf{D}^{\Delta}
-linear maps are those of the form
(f,f)
.

(iii)

In
k\text{-}\mathsf{MOD}^{\mathcal{Q}}
, the
k
-linear maps are the
k
-linear morphism
f:\mathcal{Q}(M)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}M^{\prime}
such that
{\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Q}}(f)\!:\!M\!\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\!M^{\prime}}
is a
k
-linear morphism, that is,
f(|1\rangle_{r\cdot x+s\cdot y})=r\cdot f(|1\rangle_{x})+s\cdot f(|1\rangle_{y})
, and also for pure symmetrized tensors of degree
n\geq 1
, the following equalities hold (note that in the first case, the index changes to zero on the right):

\displaystyle f(|x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle_{x_{0}})=\begin{cases}f(|x_{1}\rangle_{0})&\text{ if }n=1\\
0&\text{ if }n>1\end{cases}

The differential linear maps are the
k
-linear maps such that furthermore
f(|1\rangle_{x})=f(|x\rangle_{0})
.

5
Complete Ultrametric

In this section, we show that each hom-set of a cofree Cartesian differential category is canonically a complete ultrametric space, where the ultrametric is similar to the canonical ultrametric for formal power series or Hurwitz series
[
15
, Theorem 1.1]
. In Section
7
we will use this ultrametric to show that every map in a cofree Cartesian differential category can be decomposed into a converging infinite sum of its higher order derivatives.

The key to this ultrametric is that maps in a cofree Cartesian differential category are completely determined by the image of their higher-order derivatives in the base category.

Lemma 5.1

Let
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}\right)
be a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category over a Cartesian left
k
-linear category
\mathbb{A}
. Then for two parallel maps
f:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
and
g:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
in
\mathbb{X}
, the following are equivalent:

(i)

f=g
;

(ii)

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{n}[f]\right)=\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{n}[g]\right)
;

(iii)

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathcal{E}\left(\mathsf{D}^{n}[f]\right)=\mathcal{E}\left(\mathsf{D}^{n}[g]\right)
.

Proof:
We will prove that
(\ref{Elem1.=})\Rightarrow(\ref{Elem1.D})\Rightarrow(\ref{Elem1.partial})\Rightarrow(\ref{Elem1.=})
. Obviously,
(\ref{Elem1.=})\Rightarrow(\ref{Elem1.D})
is automatic. For
(\ref{Elem1.D})\Rightarrow(\ref{Elem1.partial})
, recall that, as discussed in Section
2
, the
n
-th derivative
\partial^{n}[f]
can be obtained by inserting zeroes into the appropriate arguments of the total
n
-th derivative
\mathsf{D}^{n}[f]
. A bit more explicitly, for a map
f:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
,
\partial^{n}[f]=\mathsf{D}^{n}[f]\circ z_{n}
, where
{z_{n}:X\times X^{\times^{n}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X^{\times^{2^{n}}}}
is the “injection” as defined in
[
14
, Lemma 3.2.(iii)]
, and so is a pairing of the projections
\pi_{j}
and zeroes
0
in the appropriate order. Note that
z_{n}
can be defined for any object in any Cartesian
k
-linear category. As such, since
\mathcal{E}
is a Cartesian
k
-linear functor, we have that
\mathcal{E}(z_{n})=z_{n}
, and thus
\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{n}[f]\right)=\mathcal{E}\left(\mathsf{D}^{n}[f]\right)\circ z_{n}
. Therefore, if for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathcal{E}\left(\mathsf{D}^{n}[f]\right)=\mathcal{E}\left(\mathsf{D}^{n}[g]\right)
, then for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{n}[f]\right)=\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{n}[g]\right)
.

For
(\ref{Elem1.partial})\Rightarrow(\ref{Elem1.=})
, we will need to do some more work. For each hom-set
\mathbb{X}(X,Y)
, define the equivalence relation
\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}}
as follows: for
f,g\in\mathbb{X}(X,Y)
,
f\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}}g
if and only if for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{n}[f]\right)=\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{n}[g]\right)
. The equivalence relation
\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}}
is compatible with the Cartesian
k
-differential structure by the properties of the higher-order partial derivative (Lemma
2.12
) in the following sense:

(i)

Faà di Bruno’s formula
[HD.5]
tells us that
\partial^{n}[g\circ f]
is a sum involving all the lower or equal degree derivatives
\partial^{m}[g]
and
\partial^{p}[f]
, where
0\leq m,p\leq n
, and some projections. Since
\mathcal{E}
preserves sums and projections, it follows that if
f_{1}\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}}g_{1}
and
f_{2}\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}}g_{2}
, then
f_{1}\circ f_{2}\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}}g_{1}\circ g_{2}
.

(ii)

By
[HD.1]
and that
\mathcal{E}
preserves the
k
-linear structure, it follows that if
f_{1}\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}}g_{1}
and
f_{2}\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}}g_{2}
, then
r\cdot f_{1}+s\cdot f_{2}\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}}r\cdot g_{1}+s\cdot g_{2}
.

(iii)

By
[HD.4]
and that
\mathcal{E}
preserves pairings, it follows that if
f_{j}\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}}g_{j}
, then
\langle f_{0},\ldots,f_{n}\rangle\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}}\langle g_{0}\ldots,g_{n}\rangle
.

(iv)

Note that
\partial^{0}\left[\mathsf{D}[f]\right]=\partial^{1}[f]
and that
[HD.8]
tells us that
\partial^{n+1}\left[\mathsf{D}[f]\right]
is the sum of
\partial^{n+2}[f]
and copies of
\partial^{n+1}[f]
, as well of some appropriate projections. Since
\mathcal{E}
preserves sums and projections, it follows that if,
f\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}}g
, then
\mathsf{D}[f]\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}}\mathsf{D}[g]
.

Now define the Cartesian
k
-differential category
\left(\mathbb{X}/\!\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}},\mathsf{D}\right)
where the objects are the same as
\mathbb{X}
and where maps are equivalence classes
\llbracket f\rrbracket
of maps
f
of
\mathbb{X}
, so the hom-sets are the quotient sets
\mathbb{X}/\!\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}}(X,Y)=\mathbb{X}(X,Y)/\!\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}}
. Composition, identities, the
k
-linear structure, the product structure, and the differential combinator are defined on representatives as in
\mathbb{X}
, which is all well-defined since
\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}}
is compatible with the Cartesian
k
-differential structure as described above.

We have a Cartesian
k
-differential functor
\mathcal{Q}_{\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}}}:\left(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}\right)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\left(\mathbb{X}/\!\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}},\mathsf{D}\right)
which is defined on objects as
\mathcal{Q}_{\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}}}(X)=X
and on maps as
\mathcal{Q}_{\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}}}(f)=\llbracket f\rrbracket
, and we also have a Cartesian
k
-linear functor
{\mathcal{E}_{\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}}}:\mathbb{X}/\!\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{A}}
which is defined on objects as
\mathcal{E}_{\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}}}(X)=\mathcal{E}(X)
and on maps
\mathcal{E}(\llbracket f\rrbracket)=\mathcal{E}(f)
. The latter functor is well-defined since if
f\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}}g
, the case
n=0
states that
\mathcal{E}(f)=\mathcal{E}(g)
. We also have that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{Q}_{\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}/\!\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}_{\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}}

Moreover, by the couniversal property of
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}\right)
, there exists a unique Cartesian
k
-differential functor
\mathcal{E}^{\flat}_{\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}}}:\left(\mathbb{X}/\!\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}},\mathsf{D}\right)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\left(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}\right)
which makes the following diagram commute:

\textstyle{\mathbb{X}/\!\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}_{\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}}}}
\scriptstyle{\exists!~\mathcal{E}^{\flat}_{\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}}

Then, by Lemma
3.3
, since the diagram on the left commutes, the diagrams on the right commutes:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[c]{c}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.31248pt\hbox{\kern 6.46802pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 33.41243pt\raise-20.72125pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.1525pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 75.74072pt\raise-28.69305pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 26.74097pt\raise 6.43999pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.865pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{Q}_{\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 66.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 66.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbb{X}/\!\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 118.58992pt\raise 7.29684pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.72186pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}^{\flat}_{\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 157.94946pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 82.20874pt\raise-24.3pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 75.74072pt\raise-31.1375pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 2.31248pt\hbox{\kern 6.46802pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.35432pt\raise 0.93512pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\hbox{\kern-0.35432pt\raise-0.93512pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.35432pt\raise
