0.93512pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\hbox{\kern-0.35432pt\raise-0.93512pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 26.74097pt\raise 6.43999pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.865pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{Q}_{\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 66.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 66.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbb{X}/\!\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 82.20874pt\raise-15.93124pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.72186pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}^{\flat}_{\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 82.20874pt\raise-24.3pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-31.1375pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 75.74072pt\raise-31.1375pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\end{array}

Now suppose that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{n}[f]\right)=\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{n}[g]\right)
. In other words,
f\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}}g
, which implies that
\mathcal{Q}_{\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}}}(f)=\mathcal{Q}_{\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}}}(g)
. By the above diagram on the right,
\mathcal{Q}_{\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}}}
is monic, and therefore,
f=g
as desired.

\Box

An ultrametric is a special kind of metric where the triangle inequality is replaced with a stronger inequality. So let
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
be the set of real non-negative numbers. An ultrametric on a set
M
is a function
\mathsf{d}:M\times M\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that for all
x,y,z\in M
the following axioms hold:

(i)

Symmetry:
\mathsf{d}(x,y)=\mathsf{d}(y,x)
;

(ii)

Non-Distinguishable:
\mathsf{d}(x,y)=0
if and only if
x=y
;

(iii)

Strong Triangle Inequality:
\mathsf{d}(x,z)\leq\mathsf{max}\{\mathsf{d}(x,y),\mathsf{d}(y,z)\}
.

An ultrametric space is a pair
(M,\mathsf{d})
consisting of a set
M
and an ultrametric
\mathsf{d}
on
M
. A complete ultrametric space is an ultrametric space where all Cauchy sequences converge. It is also useful to note that in an ultrametric space
(M,\mathsf{d})
, a sequence
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is Cauchy if and only if
\lim\limits_{n\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to9.65991pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\infty}\mathsf{d}(x_{n},x_{n+1})=0
. In the proof below, we will also need to consider the completion of an ultrametric space. For an ultrametric space
(M,\mathsf{d})
, define
\mathsf{C}[(M,\mathsf{d})]
to be the set of all Cauchy sequences
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
(M,\mathsf{d})
. Then define an equivalence relation
\thicksim
on
\mathsf{C}[(M,\mathsf{d})]
as follows:
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\thicksim(y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
if and only if
\lim\limits_{n\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to9.65991pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\infty}\mathsf{d}(x_{n},y_{n})=0
. Then define the completion of
(M,\mathsf{d})
as the quotient set
\overline{M}:=\mathsf{C}[(M,\mathsf{d})]/\thicksim
, which comes equipped with an ultrametric
\overline{\mathsf{d}}:\overline{M}\times\overline{M}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
defined as
\overline{\mathsf{d}}\left([(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}],[(y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}]\right)=\lim\limits_{n\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to9.65991pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\infty}\mathsf{d}(x_{n},y_{n})
. Then
(\overline{M},\overline{\mathsf{d}})
is a complete ultrametric space.

Proposition 5.2

Let
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}\right)
be a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category over a Cartesian left
k
-linear category
\mathbb{A}
. For each hom-set of
\mathbb{X}
, define the function
{\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}:\mathbb{X}(X,Y)\times\mathbb{X}(X,Y)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(f,g)=\begin{cases}2^{-n}&\text{ where $n\in\mathbb{N}$ is the smallest natural number such that $\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{n}[f]\right)\neq\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{n}[g]\right)$}\\
0&\text{ if all $n\in\mathbb{N}$, $\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{n}[f]\right)=\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{n}[g]\right)$}\end{cases}

Then
\left(\mathbb{X}(X,Y),\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}\right)
is a complete ultrametric space, and furthermore:

(i)

Composition is non-expansive, that is,
\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}\left(g_{1}\circ f_{1},g_{2}\circ f_{2}\right)\leq\mathsf{max}\{\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(g_{1},g_{2}),\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(f_{1},f_{2})\}
;

(ii)

Pairing is an isometry, that is,
\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}\left(\langle f_{0},\ldots,f_{n}\rangle,\langle g_{0},\ldots,g_{n}\rangle\right)=\mathsf{max}\{\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(f_{0},g_{0}),\ldots,\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(f_{n},g_{n})\}
;

(iii)

Addition and scalar multiplication is non-expansive, that is,
\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(r\cdot f_{1}+s\cdot g_{1},r\cdot f_{2}+s\cdot g_{2})\leq\mathsf{max}\{\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(f_{1},f_{2}),\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(g_{1},g_{2})\}
;

(iv)

For the differential combinator, the following equality holds:

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}\left(\mathsf{D}[f],\mathsf{D}[g]\right)=\begin{cases}2^{-n}&\text{ where $n\in\mathbb{N}$ is the smallest natural number such that $\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{n+1}[f]\right)\neq\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{n+1}[g]\right)$}\\
0&\text{ if all $n\in\mathbb{N}$, $\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{n}[f]\right)=\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{n}[g]\right)$}\end{cases}

In particular, if
\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(f,g)=0
then
\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}\left(\mathsf{D}[f],\mathsf{D}[g]\right)=0
, and if
\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(f,g)=2^{-(n+1)}
then
\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}\left(\mathsf{D}[f],\mathsf{D}[g]\right)=2^{-n}
.

(v)

A sequence
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\left(\mathbb{X}(X,Y),\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}\right)
is Cauchy if and only if for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a
N_{m}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
n\geq N_{m}
,
\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(f_{n},f_{n+1})<2^{-m}
, or equivalently,
\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{j}[f_{n}]\right)=\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{j}[f_{n+1}]\right)
for all
0\leq j\leq m
.

Proof:
We first explain why
\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}
is an ultrametric. Symmetry is automatic. The non-distinguishable axiom is precisely Lemma
5.1
. For the strong triangle inequality, consider
\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(f,g)
. If
\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(f,g)=0
then by definition for any map
h
,
0\leq\mathsf{max}\{\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(f,h),\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(h,g)\}
. If
\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(f,g)=2^{-n}
, consider another map
h
. If
\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(f,h)=0
or
\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(h,g)=0
, then
f=h
or
h=g
, so in either case we would have
\mathsf{max}\{\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(f,h),\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(h,g)\}=\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(f,g)
. So suppose that
\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(f,h)=2^{-m}
and
\mathsf{d}(h,g)=2^{-p}
. If
\mathsf{max}\{2^{-m},2^{-p}\}<2^{-n}
, then
n<\mathsf{min}\{m,p\}
which would imply that
\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{n}[f]\right)=\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{n}[h]\right)
and
\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{n}[h]\right)=\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{n}[g]\right)
. But this is a contradiction since
\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(f,g)=2^{-n}
means that
\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{n}[f]\right)\neq\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{n}[g]\right)
. So we must have that
2^{-n}\leq\mathsf{max}\{2^{-m},2^{-p}\}
as desired. So
\left(\mathbb{X}(X,Y),\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}\right)
is an ultrametric space. Next, let’s prove that the ultrametric is compatible with the Cartesian
k
-differential structure by using the identities of the higher-order partial derivative from Lemma
2.12
.

(i)

By Faà di Bruno’s formula
[HD.5]
, we have that
\partial^{n}[g\circ f]
is a sum involving all the lower or equal degree derivatives
\partial^{m}[g]
and
\partial^{p}[f]
, where
0\leq m,p\leq n
, and some projections. Clearly if
\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}\left(g_{1}\circ f_{1},g_{2}\circ f_{2}\right)=0
, then by definition we have that
0\leq\mathsf{max}\{\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(g_{1},g_{2}),\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(f_{1},f_{2})\}
. So suppose that
\mathsf{d}\left(g_{1}\circ f_{1},g_{2}\circ f_{2}\right)=2^{-n}
, which means that
\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{n}[g_{1}\circ f_{1}]\right)\neq\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{n}[g_{2}\circ f_{2}]\right)
. If
\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(g_{1},g_{2})=0
, then
g_{1}=g_{2}
, and
\mathsf{max}\{\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(g_{1},g_{2}),\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(f_{1},f_{2})\}=\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(f_{1},f_{2})
. Since
\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{n}[g_{1}\circ f_{1}]\right)\neq\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{n}[g_{1}\circ f_{2}]\right)
, it must be the case that
\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{m}[f_{1}]\right)\neq\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{m}[f_{2}]\right)
for some
m\leq n
. This implies that
2^{-n}\leq 2^{-m}\leq\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(f_{1},f_{2})=\mathsf{max}\{\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(g_{1},g_{2}),\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(f_{1},f_{2})\}
. Similarly for if
\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(f_{1},f_{2})=0
, then
2^{-n}\leq\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(g_{1},g_{2})=\mathsf{max}\{\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(g_{1},g_{2}),\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(f_{1},f_{2})\}
. Now suppose that
\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(g_{1},g_{2})=2^{-p}
and
\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(f_{1},f_{2})=2^{-m}
. If
\mathsf{max}\{2^{-m},2^{-p}\}<2^{-n}
, then
n<\mathsf{min}\{m,p\}
which would imply that for all
0\leq j\leq n
,
\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{j}[g_{1}]\right)=\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{j}[g_{2}]\right)
and
\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{j}[f_{1}]\right)=\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{j}[f_{2}]\right)
. But then, by Faà di Bruno’s formula, we would have that
\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{n}[g_{1}\circ f_{1}]\right)=\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{n}[g_{2}\circ f_{2}]\right)
, which is a contraction. So we must have that
2^{-n}\leq\mathsf{max}\{2^{-m},2^{-p}\}
as desired. So, we conclude that composition is non-expansive.

(ii)

For simplicity, let us work with pairings
\langle f_{0},f_{1}\rangle
and
\langle g_{0},g_{1}\rangle
. If
\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}\left(\langle f_{0},f_{1}\rangle,\langle g_{0},g_{1}\rangle\right)=0
then by definition
0\leq\mathsf{max}\{\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(f_{0},g_{0}),\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(f_{1},g_{1})\}
. So suppose that
\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}\left(\langle f_{0},f_{1}\rangle,\langle g_{0},g_{1}\rangle\right)=2^{-m}
. By
[HD.4]
and that
\mathcal{E}
preserves pairings, we get that:
\left\langle\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{j}[f_{0}]\right),\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{j}[f_{1}]\right)\right\rangle=\left\langle\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{j}[g_{0}]\right),\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{j}[g_{1}]\right)\right\rangle
for all
0\leq j<m
but that
\left\langle\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{m}[f_{0}]\right),\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{m}[f_{1}]\right)\right\rangle\neq\left\langle\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{m}[g_{0}]\right),\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{m}[g_{1}]\right)\right\rangle
. However, this implies that for both
i=0
and
i=1
that
\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{j}[f_{i}]\right)=\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{j}[g_{i}]\right)
for all
0\leq j<m
, but that
\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{m}[f_{0}]\right)\neq\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{m}[g_{0}]\right)
or
\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{m}[f_{1}]\right)\neq\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{m}[g_{1}]\right)
. This implies that
\mathsf{max}\{\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(f_{0},g_{0}),\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(f_{1},g_{1})\}=2^{-n}
as desired. Similarly, one can show that for arbitrary pairings that
\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}\left(\langle f_{0},\ldots,f_{n}\rangle,\langle g_{0},\ldots,g_{n}\rangle\right)=\mathsf{max}\{\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(f_{0},g_{0}),\ldots,\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(f_{n},g_{n})\}
. So, we conclude that pairing is an isometry.

(iii)

First observe that by the non-distinguishable axiom and that composition is non-expansive, it follows that for post-composition, we have that
\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(g\circ f_{1},g\circ f_{2})\leq\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(f_{1},f_{2})
. Next, observe that in any Cartesian left
k
-linear category that
r\cdot f+s\cdot g=(r\cdot\pi_{0}+s\cdot\pi_{1})\circ\langle f,g\rangle
. Using this identity, and since both post-composition and pairing are non-expansive, it follows that
\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(r\cdot f_{1}+s\cdot g_{1},r\cdot f_{2}+s\cdot g_{2})=\mathsf{max}\{\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(f_{1},f_{2}),\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(g_{1},g_{2})\}
. So, we conclude that addition and scalar multiplication are non-expansive.

(iv)

Recall that
\partial^{0}\left[\mathsf{D}[-]\right]=\partial^{1}[-]
, and by
[HD.8]
that
\partial^{n+1}\left[\mathsf{D}[-]\right]
is computed out to be a sum in terms of
\partial^{n+2}[-]
and
\partial^{n+1}[-]
, and involving some appropriate projections. Starting with the case that if all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{n}[f]\right)=\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{n}[g]\right)
, then
\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(f,g)=0
, which implies
f=g
. Then
\mathsf{D}[f]=\mathsf{D}[g]
, and thus
\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}\left(\mathsf{D}[f],\mathsf{D}[g]\right)=0
. On the other hand, if
n
is the smallest natural number such that
\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{n+1}[f]\right)\neq\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{n+1}[g]\right)
, then for all
0\leq j\leq n
that
\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{j}[f]\right)=\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{j}[g]\right)
. Then by
[HD.8]
, since
\mathcal{E}
preserves sums and projections, for all
0\leq j\leq n
we have that
\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{j}\left[\mathsf{D}[f]\right]\right)=\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{j}\left[\mathsf{D}[g]\right]\right)
but that
\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{n}\left[\mathsf{D}[f]\right]\right)\neq\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{n}\left[\mathsf{D}[g]\right]\right)
, since
\partial^{n}\left[\mathsf{D}[-]\right]
involves
\partial^{n+1}\left[-\right]
. Therefore,
\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}\left(\mathsf{D}[f],\mathsf{D}[g]\right)=2^{-n}
.

(v)

Note that
\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}\left(f,g\right)<2^{-m}
means precisely that for all
0\leq j\leq m
,
\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{j}[f]\right)=\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{j}[g]\right)
. Now for the
\Rightarrow
direction, since
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is Cauchy, for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a smallest natural number
N_{m}
such that for all
n\geq N_{m}
,
\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}\left(f_{n},f_{n+1}\right)<2^{-m}
as desired. For the
\Leftarrow
direction, let
\varepsilon>0
be an arbitrary non-negative real number and let
m
be the smallest natural number such that
2^{-m}\leq\varepsilon
. Then by assumption for all
n\geq N_{m}
,
\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}\left(f_{n},f_{n+1}\right)<2^{-m}
. As such, since we are working in an ultrametric space, we conclude that
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is Cauchy.

In order to prove that the hom-sets are also complete ultrametric spaces, let us define a new Cartesian
k
-differential category whose hom-sets are the completion of the hom-sets of
\mathbb{X}
. So define the Cartesian
k
-differential category
(\overline{\mathbb{X}},\mathsf{D})
to be the category whose objects are the same as
\mathbb{X}
and whose hom-sets are
\overline{\mathbb{X}(X,Y)}
, so a map from
X
to
Y
in
\overline{\mathbb{X}}
is an equivalence class of a Cauchy sequence
[(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}]
in
\left(\mathbb{X}(X,Y),\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}\right)
. Composition, identities, the
k
-linear structure, and the product structure are defined on representatives component-wise as in
\mathbb{X}
and the differential combinator
\mathsf{D}
is defined similarly, that is,
\mathsf{D}\left[[(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}]\right]=\left[\left(\mathsf{D}[f_{n}]\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\right]
. We need to explain why this all is well-defined.

Since non-expansive maps and isometries preserve Cauchy sequences and the equivalence relation
\thicksim
, it follows that composition, pairings, addition, and scalar multiplication are all well-defined, so
\overline{\mathbb{X}}
is indeed a Cartesian
k
-linear category. It remains to explain why the differential combinator is well-defined, that is, why the differential combinator of
\mathbb{X}
preserves Cauchy sequences and
\thicksim
. First observe that by
(iii)
, if
\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(f,g)\leq 2^{-(n+1)}
then
\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}\left(\mathsf{D}[f],\mathsf{D}[g]\right)\leq 2^{-n}
. Now let
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a Cauchy sequence in
\left(\mathbb{X}(X,Y),\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}\right)
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Then by
(v)
, we have an
N_{m+1}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
n\geq N_{m+1}
,
\mathsf{d}(f_{n},f_{n+1})<2^{-(m+1)}
. Therefore for all
n\geq N_{m+1}
,
\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}\left(\mathsf{D}[f_{n}],\mathsf{D}[f_{n+1}]\right)\leq 2^{-(m)}
, and so by
(iv)
we conclude that
\left(\mathsf{D}[f_{n}]\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Cauchy sequence in
\left(\mathbb{X}(X\times X,Y),\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}\right)
. Using a similar argument, we can also show
\mathsf{D}
preserves
\thicksim
, that is,
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\thicksim(g_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
then
\left(\mathsf{D}[f]_{n}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\thicksim\left(\mathsf{D}[g]_{n}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. Therefore, the differential combinator is well-defined, and thus
(\overline{\mathbb{X}},\mathsf{D})
is indeed a Cartesian
k
-differential category.

Now observe that by
(v)
, if
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Cauchy sequence in
\left(\mathbb{X}(X,Y),\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}\right)
, then there exists a smallest natural number
N_{0}
such that for all
n\leq N_{0}
that
\mathcal{E}(f_{n})=\mathcal{E}(f_{N_{0}})
. Therefore we obtain a Cartesian
k
-linear functor
\overline{\mathcal{E}}:\overline{\mathbb{X}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{X}
defined on objects as
\overline{\mathcal{E}}(X)
and on maps as
\overline{\mathcal{E}}\left([(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}]\right)=\mathcal{E}(f_{N_{0}})
, which indeed well-defined. By the couniversal property of
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}\right)
, there exists a unique Cartesian
k
-differential functor
{\mathcal{E}^{\flat}_{\thicksim_{\mathcal{E}}}:(\overline{\mathbb{X}},\mathsf{D})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\left(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}\right)}
which makes the following diagram commute:

\textstyle{\overline{\mathbb{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\mathcal{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\exists!~\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{\flat}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}}

We will now show that for a Cauchy sequence
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\left(\mathbb{X}(X,Y),\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}\right)
, it converges to the image of its equivalence class
\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{\flat}\left([(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}]\right)
. So let
\varepsilon>0
be an arbitrary real positive number and let
m
be the smallest natural number such that
2^{-m}\leq\varepsilon
. By
(v)
, there exists a natural number
N
such that for all
n\geq N
,
\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(f_{n},f_{n+1})<2^{-(m+1)}
, which implies that for all
0\leq j\leq m+1
that
\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{j}[f_{n}]\right)=\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{j}[f_{n+1}]\right)
. Now since
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is Cauchy,
(f_{N+n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is also a Cauchy sequence and
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\thicksim(f_{N+n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, therefore
[(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}]=[(f_{N+n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}]
. Then for all
n\geq N
and all
0\leq j\leq m+1
, we have that
\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{j}[f_{n}]\right)=\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{j}[f_{N}]\right)
and since
\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{\flat}
commutes with the derivative operators we compute that:

\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{j}\left[\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{\flat}\left([(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}]\right)\right]\right)=\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{j}\left[\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{\flat}\left([(f_{N+n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}]\right)\right]\right)=\mathcal{E}\left(\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{\flat}\left(\partial^{j}\left[[(f_{N+n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}]\right]\right)\right)=\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{j}\left[f_{N}\right]\right)=\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{j}[f_{n}]\right)

Therefore,
\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{j}\left[\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{\flat}\left([(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}]\right)\right]\right)=\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{j}[f_{n}]\right)
for all
0\leq j\leq m+1
, and so:

\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}\left(f_{n},\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{\flat}\left([(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}]\right)\right)\leq 2^{-(m+1)}<2^{-m}\leq\varepsilon

So the Cauchy sequence
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{\flat}\left([(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}]\right)
and we conclude that
\left(\mathbb{X}(X,Y),\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}\right)
is a complete ultrametric space as desired.

\Box

Remark 5.3

Complete ultrametric spaces and non-expansive maps between them form a Cartesian closed category. Proposition
5.2
says that, in fact, a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category is enriched over complete ultrametric space as a Cartesian left
k
-linear category but not as a Cartesian
k
-differential category since the differential combinator is not a non-expansive map. That said, it is easy to check that composition, the
k
-linear structure, the pairing operation, and the differential combinator are all
continuous
.

Example 5.4

Here are what the induced ultrametric and limits of Cauchy sequences are in our examples of cofree Cartesian
k
-differential categories.

(i)

For a Cartesian left
k
-linear category
\mathbb{A}
, in its Faà di Bruno construction, the distance between two Faà di Bruno sequences is given by first
n\in\mathbb{N}
where the maps in the sequence are different:

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{A}}}(f^{\bullet},g^{\bullet})=\begin{cases}2^{-n}&\text{ where $n\in\mathbb{N}$ is the smallest natural number such that $f^{n}\neq g^{n}$}\\
0&\text{ if all $n\in\mathbb{N}$, $f^{n}=g^{n}$}\end{cases}

A sequence of
(f^{\bullet}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of Faà di Bruno sequences is Cauchy if and only if the terms eventually stabilise, that is, for every
p\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a smallest natural number
N_{p}
such that for all
n\geq N_{m}
and
0\leq j\leq m
,
f^{j}_{n}=f^{j}_{n+1}
. Then a Cauchy sequence
(f^{\bullet}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to the Faà di Bruno sequence
f^{\bullet}_{N_{\bullet}}=(f^{0}_{N_{0}},f^{1}_{N_{1}},\ldots)
.

(ii)

For a
k
-linear category
\mathbb{B}
with finite biproducts, in
\mathbb{B}^{\Delta}
, there are only three possible distances between maps: none, halfway, and completely apart.

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{B}}}\left((f_{1},f_{2}),(g_{1},g_{2})\right)=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if $f_{1}\neq f_{2}$}\\
\frac{1}{2}&\text{ if $f_{1}=f_{2}$ but $g_{1}\neq g_{2}$ }\\
0&\text{ if $f_{1}=f_{2}$ and $g_{1}=g_{2}$}\end{cases}

A sequence
\left((f_{n},g_{n})\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is Cauchy if and only if it eventually repeats on forever, that is, there exists an
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
n\geq N
,
(f_{n},g_{n})=(f_{n+1},g_{n+1})
. Then a Cauchy sequence
\left((f_{n},g_{n})\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to the map it eventually repeats.

(iii)

In
k\text{-}\mathsf{MOD}^{\mathcal{Q}}
, the distance between
k
-linear morphism
f:\mathcal{Q}(M)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}M^{\prime}
and
g:\mathcal{Q}(M)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}M^{\prime}
is given by the smallest degree on which they differ on pure symmetrized tensors:

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}^{\mathcal{Q}}}(f,g)=\begin{cases}2^{-n}&\text{ where $n\in\mathbb{N}$ is the smallest natural number such that}\\
&\text{$f(|x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle_{x_{0}})\neq g(|x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle_{x_{0}})$ for some $x_{j}\in M$}\\
0&\text{ if $f=g$}\end{cases}

A sequence
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of
k
-linear morphism
f_{n}:\mathcal{Q}(M)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}M^{\prime}
is Cauchy if and only if for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a smallest natural number
N_{m}
such that for all
n\geq N_{m}
,
f_{n}
and
f_{n+1}
agree all pure symmetrized tensors of degree
0\leq j\leq m
, that is,
f_{n}(|1\rangle_{x_{0}})=f_{n+1}(|1\rangle_{x_{0}})
and
f_{n}(|x_{1},\ldots,x_{j}\rangle_{x_{0}})=f_{n+1}(|x_{1},\ldots,x_{j}\rangle_{x_{0}})
. Then a Cauchy sequence
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to the
k
-linear morphism
f:\mathcal{Q}(M)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}M^{\prime}
which is defined on pure symmetrized tensors as
f(|1\rangle_{x_{0}})=f_{N_{0}}(|1\rangle_{x_{0}})
and
f(|x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle_{x_{0}})=f_{N_{n}}(|x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle_{x_{0}})
.

6
Differential Constants

In this section, we discuss differential constants, which are maps whose derivative is zero. In particular, the main result of this section is that for a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category, the differential constants correspond precisely to the maps of the base category. Therefore, we may recapture the base category internally in a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category using its differential constants. This will allow us to provide a base independent description of cofree Cartesian
k
-differential categories in Section
7
.

Definition 6.1

In a Cartesian
k
-differential category
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
, a map
f:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
is a
differential constant
, or
\mathsf{D}
-constant for short, if
\mathsf{D}[f]=0
.

Example 6.2

Here are the differential constants in our examples of Cartesian
k
-differential categories.

(i)

In
k\text{-}\mathsf{POLY}
, every polynomial of degree zero, so a polynomial which is only a constant term, is a differential constant. If
k
is an integral domain, then the only differential constants are the degree zero polynomials. However if
k
has non-zero characteristic, it is possible to have differential constants that are not constant polynomials. For example, if
k=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, then
x^{2}
is a differential constant.

(ii)

In
\mathsf{SMOOTH}
, the only differential constants are the constant functions.

(iii)

In a
k
-linear category
\mathbb{B}
with finite biproducts, the only
\mathsf{D}^{\mathsf{lin}}
-constants are the zero maps.

(iv)

For a Cartesian left
k
-linear category
\mathbb{A}
, in its Faà di Bruno construction, the differential constants are the Faà di Bruno sequences of the form
(f,0,0,\ldots)
for any map
f
in
\mathbb{A}
.

(v)

For a
k
-linear category
\mathbb{B}
with finite biproducts, in
\mathbb{B}^{\Delta}
the
\mathsf{D}^{\Delta}
-constants are the maps of the form
(f,0)
for any map
f
in
\mathbb{B}
.

(vi)

In
k\text{-}\mathsf{MOD}^{\mathcal{Q}}
, the differential constants are the
k
-linear morphisms
f:\mathcal{Q}(M)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}M^{\prime}
such that for all pure symmetrized tensors of degree
n\geq 1
,
f\left(|x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle_{x_{0}}\right)=0
.

Here are now some basic properties of differential constants.

Lemma 6.3

In a Cartesian
k
-differential category
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})

(i)

f
is
\mathsf{D}
-constant and
\mathsf{D}
-linear if and only if
f=0
;

(ii)

If
f
or
g
is a
\mathsf{D}
-constant, then their composite
g\circ f
is a
\mathsf{D}
-constant;

(iii)

The zero maps
0
are
\mathsf{D}
-constant, and if
f
and
g
are
\mathsf{D}
-constants then for any
r,s\in k
,
r\cdot f+s\cdot g
is a
\mathsf{D}
-constant;

(iv)

If
f_{0}
, …,
f_{n}
are
\mathsf{D}
-constants, then their pairing
\langle f_{0},\ldots,f_{n}\rangle
is a
\mathsf{D}
-constant.

Furthermore, if
\mathcal{F}:(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(\mathbb{Y},\mathsf{D})
is a Cartesian
k
-differential functor, if
f
is a differential constant in
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
then
\mathcal{F}(f)
is a differential constant in
(\mathbb{Y},\mathsf{D})
.

Proof:
These are straightforward to prove, and so we leave them as an exercise for the reader.

\Box

The above lemma says that differential constants are closed under composition, addition, scalar multiplication, and pairing. Therefore, it is tempting to construct a “subcategory” of differential constants that is also Cartesian
k
-left linear. However, in general, this is not possible. Crucially, what is missing is an identity map in this “subcategory” of differential constants. Indeed, by
[CD.3]
, identity maps are not differential constants, except for the identity map of the terminal object (since, in this case, it is also a zero map). To solve this issue, we introduce a new notion called a differential constant unit, which behaves like an identity map for differential constants.

Definition 6.4

For a Cartesian
k
-differential category
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
, a
differential constant unit

\varsigma
, or
\mathsf{D}
-constant unit for short, is a family of endomorphisms indexed by objects of
\mathbb{X}
,
\varsigma=\{\varsigma_{X}:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X|~\forall X\in\mathbb{X}\}
such that
\varsigma_{X}
is a
\mathsf{D}
-constant and
\varsigma
vanishes on
\mathsf{D}
-constants, that is, for every
\mathsf{D}
-constant
f:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
,
\varsigma_{Y}\circ f=f=f\circ\varsigma_{X}
.

Lemma 6.5

For a Cartesian
k
-differential category
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
, if a
\mathsf{D}
-constant unit exists, then it is unique. Furthermore, if
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
has a
\mathsf{D}
-constant unit
\varsigma
, then:

(i)

\varsigma:1_{\mathbb{X}}\Rightarrow 1_{\mathbb{X}}
is a natural transformation, that is, for every map
f:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
,
\varsigma_{Y}\circ f=f\circ\varsigma_{X}
;

(ii)

\varsigma_{X}
is an idempotent, that is,
\varsigma_{X}\circ\varsigma_{X}=\varsigma_{X}
;

(iii)

\varsigma_{X}
is
k
-linear;

(iv)

\varsigma_{\ast}=1_{\ast}
and
\varsigma_{X_{0}\times\ldots\times X_{n}}=\varsigma_{X_{0}}\times\ldots\times\varsigma_{X_{n}}
.

Proof:
We begin by proving uniqueness of differential constant units. So suppose that
\varsigma
and
\varsigma^{\prime}
are both
\mathsf{D}
-constant units for
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
. Then
\varsigma
and
\varsigma^{\prime}
are both
\mathsf{D}
-constants and vanish on
\mathsf{D}
-constants so we have that:
\varsigma=\varsigma^{\prime}\circ\varsigma=\varsigma^{\prime}
. Therefore, we conclude that differential constants are unique. Now suppose that
\varsigma
is a
\mathsf{D}
-constant units for
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
. For any map
f
, by Lemma
6.3
.(
ii
),
\varsigma\circ f
and
f\circ\varsigma
are
\mathsf{D}
-constant. Since
\varsigma
vanishes on
\mathsf{D}
-constants, we have that:
\varsigma\circ f=\varsigma\circ f\circ\varsigma=f\circ\varsigma
. So, we conclude that
\varsigma
is a natural transformation. That
\varsigma_{X}
is an idempotent is a consequence that
\varsigma_{X}
is a
\mathsf{D}
-constant and
\varsigma
vanishes on
\mathsf{D}
-constants. While that
\varsigma_{X}
is
k
-linear follows from naturality of
\varsigma
. Lastly,
\varsigma_{\ast}=1_{\ast}
is automatic by definition of a terminal object, while
\varsigma_{X_{0}\times\ldots\times X_{n}}=\varsigma_{X_{0}}\times\ldots\times\varsigma_{X_{n}}
follows from naturality of
\varsigma
and the universal property of the product.

\Box

Using the differential constant unit we can build a category of differential constants of a Cartesian
k
-differential category. However, this will not be a subcategory since the identity maps do not correspond to the identity maps in the starting Cartesian
k
-differential category. So in particular, we do not always have an inclusion functor from this category of differential constants back to the Cartesian
k
-differential category.

Lemma 6.6

Let
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
be a Cartesian
k
-differential category with a
\mathsf{D}
-constant unit
\varsigma
. Then
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]
is a Cartesian left
k
-linear category where:

(i)

The objects of
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]
are the same as
\mathbb{X}
;

(ii)

The maps in
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]
are differential constants in
\mathbb{X}
;

(iii)

Composition is defined as in
\mathbb{X}
;

(iv)

The identity of
X
in
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]
is the
\mathsf{D}
-constant unit
\varsigma_{X}:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X
;

(v)

The terminal object and the product of objects are the same as in
\mathbb{X}
;

(vi)

The projections in
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]
are
\varsigma_{X_{j}}\circ\pi_{j}:X_{0}\times\ldots\times X_{n}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X_{j}
and where the pairing is as in
\mathbb{X}
;

(vii)

The
k
-linear structure is defined as in
\mathbb{X}
.

Furthermore, the functor
\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}:\mathbb{X}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]
defined on objects as
\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}(X)=X
and on maps as
\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}(f)=\varsigma\circ f
, is a Cartesian
k
-linear functor.

Proof:
Since
\varsigma
is a
\mathsf{D}
-constant and vanish on
\mathsf{D}
-constants, it is a well-defined identity in
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]
, and so
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]
is indeed a category. The
k
-linear structure is well-defined by Lemma
6.3
.(
iii
), and since
\mathbb{X}
is a left
k
-linear category, so is
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]
. By Lemma
6.3
.(
ii
), the projections are well-defined and since both
\varsigma
and
\pi_{j}
are
k
-linear, their composite
\varsigma\circ\pi_{j}
is also
k
-linear. By Lemma
6.3
.(
iv
) the pairing is well-defined, and since
\varsigma
vanishes on
\mathsf{D}
-constants, will satisfy the necessary universal property since it does so in
\mathbb{X}
. So we conclude that
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]
is a Cartesian left
k
-linear category. Lastly, since
\varsigma
is idempotent and natural,
\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}:\mathbb{X}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]
is indeed a functor, and it is clear that it is Cartesian
k
-linear.

\Box

We will now prove that every cofree Cartesian
k
-linear category has a differential constant unit and that the base category is isomorphic to its category of differential constants. To do so we will need the following useful lemma that the functor down to the base category is bijective on differential constants.

Lemma 6.7

Let
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}\right)
be a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category over a Cartesian left
k
-linear category
\mathbb{A}
. If
f
and
g
are parallel differential constants in
\mathbb{X}
, then
f=g
if and only if
\mathcal{E}(f)=\mathcal{E}(g)
.

Proof:
The
\Rightarrow
direction is automatic. For the
\Leftarrow
direction, suppose that
\mathcal{E}(f)=\mathcal{E}(g)
. Since
f
and
g
are
\mathsf{D}
-constants, then for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\partial^{n+1}[f]=0=\partial^{n+1}[g]
. Then for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{n}[f]\right)=\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{n}[g]\right)
. Then by Lemma
5.1
,
f=g
.

\Box

Proposition 6.8

Let
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}\right)
be a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category over a Cartesian left
k
-linear category
\mathbb{A}
. Then
\mathbb{X}
has a differential constant unit
\varsigma
such that
\mathcal{E}(\varsigma_{X})=1_{\mathcal{E}(X)}
. Furthermore, the functor
\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{\varsigma}:\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{A}
, defined on objects and maps as
\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{\varsigma}(-)=\mathcal{E}(-)
, is a Cartesian
k
-linear isomorphism, so
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\cong\mathbb{A}
, and it is the unique Cartesian
k
-linear isomorphism such that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\exists!~\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{\varsigma}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}}

In other words, for every map
f
in
\mathbb{A}
, there exists a unique
\mathsf{D}
-constant
f^{\sharp}
in
\mathbb{X}
such that
\mathcal{E}(f^{\sharp})=f
.

Proof:
Consider the Cartesian
k
-differential category
\left(\mathbb{X}^{\Delta},\mathsf{D}^{\Delta}\right)
and Cartesian
k
-linear functor
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{X}}:\mathbb{X}^{\Delta}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{X}
as defined in Lemma
3.17
. Therefore by the couniversal property of
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}\right)
, there exists a unique Cartesian
k
-differential functor
\mathcal{R}:\left(\mathbb{X}^{\Delta},\mathsf{D}^{\Delta}\right)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\left(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}\right)
such that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\mathbb{X}^{\Delta}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\exists!~\mathcal{R}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}}

Note that on objects,
\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{R}(X))=\mathcal{E}(X)
. By Corollary
3.13
,
\mathcal{E}
is bijective on objects, and therefore
\mathcal{R}(X)=X
. Then define
\varsigma_{X}:1_{X}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}1_{X}
as
\varsigma_{X}:=\mathcal{R}(1_{X},0)
. First, note that by definition:

\mathcal{E}(\varsigma_{X})=\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{R}(1_{X},0))=\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{X}}(1_{X},0))=\mathcal{E}(1_{X})=1_{\mathcal{E}(X)}

So
\mathcal{E}(\varsigma_{X})=1_{\mathcal{E}(X)}
as desired. Next observe that
(1_{X},0)
is a differential constant in
\mathbb{X}^{\Delta}
, and then by Lemma
6.3
,
\varsigma_{X}
is also a differential constant. Now, for any differential constant
f:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
in
\mathbb{X}
, note that
\varsigma_{Y}\circ f
and
f\circ\varsigma_{X}
are also differential constants. Since
\mathcal{E}(\varsigma_{X})=1_{\mathcal{E}(X)}
, it follows that
\mathcal{E}(\varsigma_{Y}\circ f)=\mathcal{E}(f)=\mathcal{E}(f\circ\varsigma_{X})
. Then, by Lemma
6.7
, we have that
\varsigma_{Y}\circ f=f=f\circ\varsigma_{X}
. So, we conclude that
\varsigma
is a differential constant unit.

Now
\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{\varsigma}:\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{A}
will be a Cartesian
k
-linear functor since
\mathcal{E}
is. On objects,
\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{\varsigma}(\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}(X))=\mathcal{E}(X)
, while on maps, since
\mathcal{E}(\varsigma_{X})=1_{\mathcal{E}(X)}
, it follows by definition that
\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{\varsigma}(\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}(f))=\mathcal{E}(f)
. Therefore,
\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{\varsigma}\circ\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}=\mathcal{E}
. Uniqueness follows from the fact that if
f
is differential constant, then
\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}(f)=f
. To show that
\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{\varsigma}
is an isomorphism, it is sufficient to show that it is bijective on objects and maps. By Corollary
3.13
,
\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{\varsigma}
is bijective on objects by definition. On the other hand, we will show that
\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{\varsigma}
is injective and surjective on maps. That
\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{\varsigma}
is injective on maps follows from Lemma
6.7
. For surjectivity, let
f:A\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}B
be a map in
\mathbb{A}
. Consider the Cartesian
k
-differential category
\left(\mathbb{A}^{\Delta},\mathsf{D}^{\Delta}\right)
and Cartesian
k
-linear functor
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{A}}:\mathbb{A}^{\Delta}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{A}
as defined in Lemma
3.17
. Therefore by the couniversal property of
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}\right)
, there exists a unique Cartesian
k
-differential functor
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{A}}^{\flat}:\left(\mathbb{X}^{\Delta},\mathsf{D}^{\Delta}\right)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\left(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}\right)
such that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\mathbb{A}^{\Delta}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\exists!~\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{A}}^{\flat}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}}

Note that
(f,0)
is a differential constant in
\mathbb{A}^{\Delta}
, and so by Lemma
6.3
,
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{B}}^{\flat}(f,0)
is also a differential constant in
\mathbb{X}
. So
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{X}}^{\flat}(f,0)
is a map in
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]
. By definition we have that
\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{\varsigma}\left(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{A}}^{\flat}(f,0)\right)=f
. As such, we have that
\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{\varsigma}
is surjective on maps, and thus
\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{\varsigma}
is bijective on maps. So we get that
\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{\varsigma}
is an isomorphism as desired. Explicitly, the inverse
\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{-1}_{\varsigma}:\mathbb{A}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]
is defined on objects as
\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{-1}_{\varsigma}(A)=\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{A}}^{\flat}(A)
and on maps as
\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{-1}_{\varsigma}(f)=\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{A}}^{\flat}(f,0)
. Therefore, we conclude that
\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{\varsigma}
is a Cartesian
k
-linear isomorphism, and so
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\cong\mathbb{A}
.

\Box

Remark 6.9

Note that since differential constant units are unique, the differential constant unit of a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category is independent of the base Cartesian left
k
-linear category.

Example 6.10

Here are the induced differential constant units for our examples of cofree Cartesian
k
-differential categories.

(i)

For a Cartesian left
k
-linear category
\mathbb{A}
, in its Faà di Bruno construction, the differential constant unit is the Faà di Bruno sequence
\varsigma^{\bullet}_{X}:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}X
defined as the identity map in the first term and zero for the rest of the sequence,
\varsigma^{\bullet}:=(1_{X},0,0,\ldots)
.

(ii)

For a
k
-linear category
\mathbb{B}
with finite biproducts, in
\mathbb{B}^{\Delta}
, the
\mathsf{D}^{\Delta}
-constant unit is the pair of an identity map first and then a zero map,
\varsigma_{A}:=(1_{A},0)
.

(iii)

In
k\text{-}\mathsf{MOD}^{\mathcal{Q}}
, the differential constant unit is the
k
-linear morphism
\varsigma_{M}:\mathcal{Q}(M)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}M
defined on pure symmetrized tensors as
\varsigma_{M}\left(|1\rangle_{(x_{0})}\right)=x_{0}
for degree
0
and
\varsigma_{M}\left(|x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle_{x_{0}}\right)=0
for all degrees
n\geq 1
.

Example 6.11

We may now also explain why the Cartesian
k
-differential categories given by differentiating smooth functions, polynomials, and linear maps are not cofree. Indeed, recall that in all three of these examples, the differential linear maps are the same thing as
k
-linear maps. However, Lemma
6.5
.(
iii
) tells us that differential constant units are always
k
-linear, while Lemma
6.3
.(
i
) tell us that the only differential constants that are also differential linear are the zero maps. So, in these examples, if a differential constant unit existed, then it would be differential linear and, therefore, must be zero. However, if a differential constant unit is zero, by the fact that it vanishes on differential constants, it follows that all differential constants would also have to be zero in this case.

(i)

In
k\text{-}\mathsf{POLY}
, note that the multiplicative unit
1
is always a differential constant. Therefore if
k
is non-trivial, so
0\neq 1
, then
k\text{-}\mathsf{POLY}
will have non-zero differential constants. As such, as explained above,
k\text{-}\mathsf{POLY}
cannot have a differential constant unit. Thus
k\text{-}\mathsf{POLY}
is not cofree.

(ii)

Similarly,
\mathsf{SMOOTH}
has non-zero differential constants and, therefore, cannot have a differential constant unit. Thus
\mathsf{SMOOTH}
is not cofree.

(iii)

Any
k
-linear category
\mathbb{B}
with finite biproducts does have a differential constant unit given by
0
, and so
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{B}\right]
is equivalent to the terminal category
\ast
, which has only one object and one map. However, the Faà di Bruno construction over
\ast
is precisely
\ast
. Therefore if
\mathbb{B}
is non-trivial, that is, has at least one non-zero object, then
\mathbb{B}
is not equivalent to
\ast
and therefore cannot be cofree.

We are finally in a position to show that for cofree Cartesian
k
-differential categories, base categories are unique up to isomorphism and that being a base is transferable through isomorphisms.

Lemma 6.12

Let
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{A}^{\prime}
be Cartesian left
k
-linear categories, and let
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
be a Cartesian
k
-differential category. Then:

(i)

If
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}\right)
is a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category over
\mathbb{A}
and
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\right)
is a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category over
\mathbb{A}^{\prime}
, then there exists a unique Cartesian
k
-linear isomorphism
\mathcal{A}:\mathbb{A}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{A}^{\prime}
, so
\mathbb{A}\cong\mathbb{A}^{\prime}
, such that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\exists!~\mathcal{A}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}^{\prime}}

(ii)

If
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}\right)
is a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category over
\mathbb{A}
and there exists a Cartesian
k
-linear isomorphism
\mathcal{A}:\mathbb{A}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{A}^{\prime}
, so
\mathbb{A}\cong\mathbb{A}^{\prime}
, then
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{A}\circ\mathcal{E}\right)
is a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category over
\mathbb{A}^{\prime}
.

Proof:
For
(i)
, by Proposition
6.8
, we have that
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
has a differential constant unit
\varsigma
and also that
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\cong\mathbb{A}
and
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\cong\mathbb{A}^{\prime}
as Cartesian left
k
-linear categories. So define
\mathcal{A}:\mathbb{A}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{A}^{\prime}
as the Cartesian left
k
-linear isomorphism given by
\mathbb{A}\cong\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\cong\mathbb{A}^{\prime}
. By Proposition
6.8
, this isomorphism is the unique isomorphism which is compatible with
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
in the desired way. For
(ii)
, let
(\mathbb{Y},\mathsf{D})
be a Cartesian
k
-differential category and
{\mathcal{F}:\mathbb{Y}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{A}^{\prime}}
a Cartesian
k
-linear functor. Using the couniversal property of
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}\right)
, consider the induced unique Cartesian
k
-differential functor
(\mathcal{A}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{F}^{\flat}):(\mathbb{Y},\mathsf{D})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
which makes the diagram on the left commute, which implies the diagram on the right commutes:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[c]{c}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.31248pt\hbox{\kern 10.95244pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.46802pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbb{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-10.95244pt\raise-14.3629pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.1525pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-23.41333pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 74.33812pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 74.33812pt\raise-45.2004pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.1525pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{A}}$}}}\kern
