Uniqueness follows from by Lemma
3.3
. Thus
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{A}\circ\mathcal{E}\right)
is a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category over
\mathbb{A}^{\prime}
.

\Box

As such, we may now provide another characterisation of cofree Cartesian
k
-differential categories as having a differential constant and being cofree over their category of differential constants.

Theorem 6.13

For a Cartesian
k
-differential category
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
, the following are equivalent:

(i)

(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
is cofree;

(ii)

(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
has a
\mathsf{D}
-constant unit
\varsigma
such that
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}\right)
is a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category over
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]
;

(iii)

(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
has a
\mathsf{D}
-constant unit
\varsigma
and the unique Cartesian
k
-differential functor
\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}:(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(\mathsf{F}\left[\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\right],\mathsf{D})
which makes the following diagram commute

\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}}
\scriptstyle{\exists!~\mathcal{E}^{\flat}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{F}\left[\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]}

(which is explicitly defined on objects as
\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}(X)=X
and on maps as
\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}(f)=\left(\varsigma\circ\partial^{n}[f]\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
), is an isomorphism, so
\mathbb{X}\cong\mathsf{F}\left[\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\right]
.

Proof:
Observe that
(ii)\Leftrightarrow(iii)
is a specific case of Lemma
3.11
. Therefore, it suffices to prove that
(i)\Leftrightarrow(ii)
. By definition,
(ii)\Rightarrow(i)
is automatic. For
(i)\Rightarrow(ii)
, let
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}\right)
be a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category over a Cartesian left
k
-linear category
\mathbb{A}
. By Proposition
6.8
,
\mathbb{X}
has a differential constant unit
\varsigma
and
\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{\varsigma}:\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{A}
is a Cartesian
k
-linear isomorphism, such that
\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{\varsigma}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}
. Then by Lemma
6.12
.(
ii
),
(\mathbb{X},\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma})
is a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category over
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]
.

\Box

Theorem
6.13
.(
iii
) will be particularly useful in the proofs of Theorem
7.5
and Theorem
8.6
, which provide base independent characterisations of cofree Cartesian
k
-differential categories. We can also apply the results of Section
4
to describe the differential linear and
k
-linear maps of a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category as
k
-linear differential constants.

Corollary 6.14

Let
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
be a Cartesian
k
-differential category which is cofree and with
\mathsf{D}
-constant unit
\varsigma
. Then
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\cong k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\right]
and
k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\cong k\text{-}\mathsf{lin}\left[\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\right]^{\Delta}
.

7
Characterisation via Differential Constants

By Theorem
6.13
, a Cartesian
k
-differential category is cofree if and only if it has a differential constant unit and is cofree over its category of differential constants. This characterisation still requires one to check the couniversal property, and therefore we have to work “outside” of the Cartesian
k
-differential category. In this section, we provide necessary and sufficient conditions on differential constants to give us the desired couniversal property. In particular, this provides a base independent and completely internal characterisation of cofree Cartesian
k
-differential categories.

As explained in Section
5
, the hom-sets of a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential categories have a canonical ultrametric induced by the functor down to the base category. To provide a base-independent description of the ultrametric, we may re-express it using the differential constant unit. This ultrametric property can be defined in a Cartesian
k
-differential category with a differential constant unit, which we call being differential constant complete.

Definition 7.1

A Cartesian
k
-differential category
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
with a
\mathsf{D}
-constant unit
\varsigma
is said to be
differential constant complete
, or
\mathsf{D}
-constant complete for short, if for each hom-set the function
{\mathsf{d}_{\varsigma}:\mathbb{X}(X,Y)\times\mathbb{X}(X,Y)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}
defined as follows:

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}_{\varsigma}(f,g)=\begin{cases}2^{-n}&\text{ where $n\in\mathbb{N}$ is the smallest natural number such that $\varsigma_{Y}\circ\partial^{n}[f]\neq\varsigma_{Y}\circ\partial^{n}[g]$}\\
0&\text{ if all $n\in\mathbb{N}$, $\varsigma_{Y}\circ\partial^{n}[f]=\varsigma_{Y}\circ\partial{D}^{n}[g]$}\end{cases}

is an ultrametric on
\mathbb{X}(X,Y)
such that
(\mathbb{X}(X,Y),\mathsf{d}_{\varsigma})
is a complete ultrametric space.

Lemma 7.2

Let
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}\right)
be a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category over a Cartesian left
k
-linear category
\mathbb{A}
, and let
\varsigma
be the induced
\mathsf{D}
-constant unit. Then
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
is
\mathsf{D}
-constant complete and furthermore,
\mathsf{d}_{\varsigma}(-,-)=\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(-,-)
.

Proof:
It suffices to prove that
\mathsf{d}_{\varsigma}(-,-)=\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}(-,-)
. By Proposition
6.8
,
\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{\varsigma}\circ\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}=\mathcal{E}
and
\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{\varsigma}
is an isomorphism, and so in particular injective on maps. Therefore, we have that:

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}_{\mathcal{E}}\left(f,g\right)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}2^{-n}&\text{ where $n\in\mathbb{N}$ is the smallest natural number such that $\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{n}[f]\right)\neq\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{n}[g]\right)$}\\
0&\text{ if all $n\in\mathbb{N}$, $\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{n}[f]\right)=\mathcal{E}\left(\partial^{n}[g]\right)$}\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}2^{-n}&\text{ where $n\in\mathbb{N}$ is the smallest natural number such that $\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{\varsigma}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}\left(\partial^{n}[f]\right)\right)\neq\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{\varsigma}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}\left(\partial^{n}[g]\right)\right)$}\\
0&\text{ if all $n\in\mathbb{N}$, $\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{\varsigma}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}\left(\partial^{n}[f]\right)\right)=\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{\varsigma}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}\left(\partial^{n}[g]\right)\right)$}\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}2^{-n}&\text{ where $n\in\mathbb{N}$ is the smallest natural number such that $\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{\varsigma}\left(\varsigma\circ\partial^{n}[f]\right)\neq\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{\varsigma}\left(\varsigma\circ\partial^{n}[g]\right)$}\\
0&\text{ if all $n\in\mathbb{N}$, $\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{\varsigma}\left(\varsigma\circ\partial^{n}[f]\right)=\overline{\mathcal{E}}_{\varsigma}\left(\varsigma\circ\partial^{n}[g]\right)$}\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}2^{-n}&\text{ where $n\in\mathbb{N}$ is the smallest natural number such that $\varsigma_{Y}\circ\partial^{n}[f]\neq\varsigma_{Y}\circ\partial^{n}[g]$}\\
0&\text{ if all $n\in\mathbb{N}$, $\varsigma_{Y}\circ\partial^{n}[f]=\varsigma_{Y}\circ\partial{D}^{n}[g]$}\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{d}_{\varsigma}(f,g)

Then we conclude that
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
is
\mathsf{D}
-constant complete.

\Box

We also require the ability to express maps in terms of converging infinite sums based on their higher-order derivatives, which, in a way, means that maps are formal power series or Hurwitz series
[
15
]
.

Definition 7.3

In a Cartesian
k
-differential category
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
with a differential constant unit
\varsigma
, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, a
differential constant homogeneous map of degree
n
, or
\mathsf{D}
-constant homogenous map for short, is a map
{f:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y}
such that
\varsigma_{Y}\circ\partial^{m}[f]=0
for all
m\neq n
. A Cartesian
k
-differential category
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
with a differential constant unit
\varsigma
is said to have
convenient differential constants
, or
convenient
\mathsf{D}
-constants for short, if for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and every differential constant
f:X\times X^{\times^{n}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
which is
k
-multilinear and symmetric in its last
n
-arguments, there exists a unique map differential constant homogeneous map of degree
n
,
f^{\natural}:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
, such that
\varsigma_{Y}\circ\partial^{n}[f^{\natural}]=f
.

Lemma 7.4

Let
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
be a Cartesian
k
-differential category with a
\mathsf{D}
-constant unit
\varsigma
which is
\mathsf{D}
-constant complete and also has convenient
\mathsf{D}
-constants.

(i)

Suppose that
f^{\bullet}:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
is a
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]
-Faà di Bruno sequence, that is, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}

f^{n}:X\times X^{\times^{n}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
is a
\mathsf{D}
-constant which
k
-multilinear and symmetric in its last
n
-arguments. For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, consider the unique
\mathsf{D}
-constant homogenous map
{f^{n}}^{\natural}:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
such that
\varsigma_{Y}\circ\partial^{n}[{f^{n}}^{\natural}]=f^{n}
. Then the series
\sum\limits^{\infty}_{n=0}{f^{n}}^{\natural}
converges in
(\mathbb{X}(X,Y),\mathsf{d}_{\varsigma})
, and furthermore, for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
\varsigma_{Y}\circ\partial^{m}\left[\sum\limits^{\infty}_{n=0}{f^{n}}^{\natural}\right]=f^{m}
.

(ii)

For any map
f:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
, define
f_{(n)}:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
to be
f_{(n)}=(\varsigma_{Y}\circ\partial^{n}[f])^{\natural}
, that is,
f_{(n)}
is the unique map such that
\varsigma_{Y}\circ\partial^{n}[f_{(n)}]=\varsigma_{Y}\circ\partial^{n}[f]
and
\varsigma_{Y}\circ\partial^{m}[f_{(n)}]=0
for all
m\neq n
. Then
f_{(0)}=\varsigma_{Y}\circ f
and the series
\sum\limits^{\infty}_{n=0}f_{(n)}
converges in
(\mathbb{X}(X,Y),\mathsf{d}_{\varsigma})
to
f
, that is,
\sum\limits^{\infty}_{n=0}f_{(n)}=f
.

Proof:
For (
i
), since
(\mathbb{X}(X,Y),\mathsf{d}_{\varsigma})
is a complete metric space, to show that the series converges it suffices to show that the sequence
\left(\sum\limits^{m}_{n=0}{f^{n}}^{\natural}\right)_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
is Cauchy. Note that in this case, a sequence
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\left(\mathbb{X}(X,Y),\mathsf{d}_{\varsigma}\right)
is Cauchy if and only if for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a
N_{m}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
n\geq N_{m}
,
\mathsf{d}_{\varsigma}(f_{n},f_{n+1})<2^{-m}
, or equivalently,
\varsigma_{Y}\circ\partial^{j}[f_{n}]=\varsigma_{Y}\circ\partial^{j}[f_{n+1}]
for all
0\leq j\leq m
. Now for any natural number
p\in\mathbb{N}
, for all
m\geq p
, by homogeneity and
[HD.1]
, we have that for any
0\leq j\leq p
that
\varsigma_{Y}\circ\partial^{j}\left[\sum\limits^{m}_{n=0}{f^{n}}^{\natural}\right]=f^{j}
. Therefore, for all
m\geq p
,
\mathsf{d}_{\varsigma}\left(\sum\limits^{m}_{n=0}{f^{n}}^{\natural},\sum\limits^{m}_{n=0}{f^{m}+1}^{\natural}\right)<2^{-p}
. Thus it follows that
\left(\sum\limits^{m}_{n=0}{f^{n}}^{\natural}\right)_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
is Cauchy and therefore the series
\sum\limits^{\infty}_{n=0}{f^{n}}^{\natural}
converges in
(\mathbb{X}(X,Y),\mathsf{d}_{\varsigma})
.

To show the other desired identity, we first need to explain why the differential combinator preserves infinite sums. By similar arguments that were done in the proof of Proposition
5.2
, we note that if
\mathsf{d}_{\varsigma}(f,g)\leq 2^{-(n+1)}
then
\mathsf{d}_{\varsigma}\left(\mathsf{D}[f],\mathsf{D}[g]\right)\leq 2^{-n}
. Now suppose that
\sum\limits^{\infty}_{n=0}f_{n}
is a converging series. We must show that
\mathsf{D}\left[\sum\limits^{\infty}_{n=0}f_{n}\right]
is the limit of the sequence
\left(\sum\limits^{m}_{n=0}\mathsf{D}[f_{n}]\right)_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
. So let
\varepsilon>0
be an arbitrary non-zero positive real number and let
p
be the smallest natural number such that
2^{-p}\leq\varepsilon
. Since
\sum\limits^{\infty}_{n=0}f_{n}
converges, there exists a natural number
N
such that for all
m\geq N
,
\mathsf{d}_{\varsigma}\left(\sum\limits^{m}_{n=0}f_{n},\sum\limits^{\infty}_{n=0}f_{n}\right)<2^{-(p+2)}
. So we have that
\mathsf{d}_{\varsigma}\left(\mathsf{D}\left[\sum\limits^{m}_{n=0}f_{n}\right],\mathsf{D}\left[\sum\limits^{\infty}_{n=0}f_{n}\right]\right)\leq 2^{-(p+1)}
. By
[CD.2]
,
\mathsf{D}\left[\sum\limits^{m}_{n=0}f_{n}\right]=\sum\limits^{m}_{n=0}\mathsf{D}\left[f_{n}\right]
. Therefore, we have that
\mathsf{d}_{\varsigma}\left(\sum\limits^{m}_{n=0}\mathsf{D}\left[f_{n}\right],\mathsf{D}\left[\sum\limits^{\infty}_{n=0}f_{n}\right]\right)\leq 2^{-(p+1)}<2^{-p}\leq\varepsilon
. So
\sum\limits^{\infty}_{n=0}\mathsf{D}\left[f_{n}\right]=\mathsf{D}\left[\sum\limits^{\infty}_{n=0}f_{n}\right]
. Similarly, we can show that
\partial^{m}
also preserves infinite sums.

As such, we finally have that for all
m\in\mathbb{N}

\varsigma_{Y}\circ\partial^{m}\left[\sum\limits^{\infty}_{n=0}{f^{n}}^{\natural}\right]=\varsigma_{Y}\circ\sum\limits^{\infty}_{n=0}\partial^{m}\left[{f^{n}}^{\natural}\right]=\varsigma_{Y}\circ(0+0+\ldots+0+f^{m}+0+0+\ldots)=\varsigma_{Y}\circ f^{m}=f^{m}

So
\varsigma_{Y}\circ\partial^{m}\left[\sum\limits^{\infty}_{n=0}{f^{n}}^{\natural}\right]=f^{m}
as desired.

For (
ii
), note that
\left(\varsigma_{Y}\circ\partial^{n}[f]\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]
-Faà di Bruno sequence. So by (
i
) the series
\sum\limits^{\infty}_{n=0}f_{(n)}
converges. Next note that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, by (
i
) we also have that,
\varsigma_{Y}\circ\partial^{n}\left[\sum\limits^{\infty}_{n=0}{f_{(n)}}^{\natural}\right]=\varsigma_{Y}\circ\partial^{n}[f]
. But this implies that
\mathsf{d}_{\varsigma}\left(\sum\limits^{\infty}_{n=0}{f_{(n)}}^{\natural},f\right)=0
. Since
\mathsf{d}_{\varsigma}
is an ultrametric, we conclude that
\sum\limits^{\infty}_{n=0}f_{(n)}=f
.

\Box

We may now state the main result of this section:

Theorem 7.5

A Cartesian
k
-differential category is cofree if and only if it has a differential constant unit, is differential constant complete, and has convenient differential constants.

Proof:
For the
\Rightarrow
direction, suppose that
\left(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}\right)
is a Cartesian
k
-differential category which is cofree. By Theorem
6.13
, this implies that
\left(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}\right)
has a
\mathsf{D}
-constant unit
\varsigma
and the unique induced Cartesian
k
-differential functor
{\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}:\left(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}\right)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\left(\mathsf{F}\left[\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\right],\mathsf{D}\right)}
is an isomorphism. By Lemma
7.2
, we already have that
\left(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}\right)
is
\mathsf{D}
-constant complete. So it suffices to prove that
\left(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}\right)
also has convenient
\mathsf{D}
-constants. First observe that by the couniversal property of
\left(\left(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}\right),\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}\right)
, that we have a unique Cartesian
k
-differential functor
{\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}}^{-1}:\left(\mathsf{F}\left[\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\right],\mathsf{D}\right)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\left(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}\right)
which makes the following diagram commute:

\textstyle{\mathsf{F}\left[\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]}}
\scriptstyle{\exists!~{\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}}^{-1}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}}

Then it follows that on objects
{\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}}^{-1}(X)=X
and that for a
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]
-Faà di Bruno sequence
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
, the following equality holds for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\varsigma_{Y}\circ{\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}}^{-1}\left((f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\right)=f_{0}
. Now let
f:X\times X^{\times^{n}}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
be a differential constant map which is also
k
-multilinear and symmetric in its last
n
-arguments. Then the sequence
(f^{\sharp}_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
defined as
f^{\sharp}_{n}=f_{n}
and
f^{\sharp}_{m}=0
if
m\neq n
, is a
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]
-Faà di Bruno sequence. So
(f^{\sharp}_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
is a map in
\mathsf{F}\left[\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\right]
and therefore
{\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}}^{-1}\left((f^{\sharp}_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\right):X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
is a map in
\mathbb{X}
. So define
f^{\natural}:={\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}}^{-1}\left((f^{\sharp}_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\right)
. Then we compute:

\varsigma_{Y}\circ\partial^{n}[f^{\natural}]=\varsigma_{Y}\circ\partial^{n}\left[{\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}}^{-1}\left((f^{\sharp}_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\right)\right]=\varsigma_{Y}\circ{\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}}^{-1}\left(\partial^{n}\left[(f^{\sharp}_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\right]\right)=\partial^{n}\left[(f^{\sharp}_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\right]_{0}=\begin{cases}f_{n}&\text{ if }m=n\\
0&\text{ if }m\neq n\end{cases}

So
\varsigma_{Y}\circ\partial^{n}[f^{\natural}]=f
and
\varsigma_{Y}\circ\partial^{m}[f^{\natural}]=0
for all
m\neq n
as desired. For uniqueness, suppose that there is another map
h:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
such that
\varsigma_{Y}\circ\partial^{n}[h]=f
and
\varsigma_{Y}\circ\partial^{m}[h]=0
for all
m\neq n
. By definition of
\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}
, as given in Theorem
6.13
, it follows that
\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}(h)=(f^{\sharp}_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
. Thus,
h={\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}}^{-1}\left((f^{\sharp}_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\right)
, and so
f^{\natural}
is unique. Therefore we conclude that
\left(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}\right)
has convenient
\mathsf{D}
-constants.

For the
\Leftarrow
direction, suppose that
\left(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}\right)
is a Cartesian
k
-differential category which has a
\mathsf{D}
-constant unit
\varsigma
, and which is
\mathsf{D}
-constant complete and has convenient
\mathsf{D}
-constants. Then by Theorem
6.13
, to prove that
\left(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}\right)
is cofree, it suffices to prove that the unique induced Cartesian
k
-differential functor
{\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}:\left(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}\right)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\left(\mathsf{F}\left[\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\right],\mathsf{D}\right)}
is an isomorphism. To do so, it suffices to prove that
\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}
is bijective on objects and maps, allowing us to avoid working with the composition in
\mathsf{F}\left[\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\right]
. By construction,
\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}
is bijective on objects via Corollary
3.13
. Then for maps, starting with injectivity, suppose that
\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}(f)=\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}(g)
. This implies that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\varsigma\circ\partial^{n}[f]=\varsigma\circ\partial^{n}[g]
. By Lemma
7.4
.(
ii
), we have that:

f=\sum\limits^{\infty}_{n=0}f_{(n)}=\sum\limits^{\infty}_{n=0}(\varsigma\circ\partial^{n}[f])^{\natural}=\sum\limits^{\infty}_{n=0}(\varsigma\circ\partial^{n}[g])^{\natural}=\sum\limits^{\infty}_{n=0}g_{(n)}=g

So
f=g
, thus
\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}
is injective on maps. For surjectivity, let
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]
-Faà di Bruno sequence. By Lemma
7.4
.(
i
), we have that the series
\sum\limits^{\infty}_{n=0}f^{\natural}_{n}
converges (and is a map in
\mathbb{X}
), and we compute:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}\left(\sum\limits^{\infty}_{n=0}f^{\natural}_{n}\right)=\left(\varsigma\circ\partial^{n}\left[\sum\limits^{\infty}_{n=0}f^{\natural}_{n}\right]\right)_{n\in\mathbb{X}}=(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

So
\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}
is surjective. Thus,
\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}
is bijective on maps, and we conclude that
\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}
is an isomorphism. Explicitly, the inverse
{\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}}^{-1}:\mathsf{F}\left[\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\right]\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{X}
is defined on objects as
{\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}}^{-1}(X)=X
and on maps as
{\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}}^{-1}\left((f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\right)=\sum\limits^{\infty}_{n=0}f^{\natural}_{n}
. Therefore, we conclude that
\mathbb{X}
is cofree.

\Box

We now explicitly express the couniversal property of a Cartesian
k
-differential category which has a differential constant unit, is differential constant complete, and has convenient differential constants.

Corollary 7.6

Let
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
be a Cartesian
k
-differential category which has a
\mathsf{D}
-constant unit
\varsigma
, and which is
\mathsf{D}
-constant complete and has convenient
\mathsf{D}
-constants. Then

\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}\right)
is a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category over
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]
, where in particular for any Cartesian
k
-differential category
(\mathbb{Y},\mathsf{D})
and Cartesian
k
-linear functor
{\mathcal{F}:\mathbb{Y}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{A}}
, the unique Cartesian
k
-differential functor
\mathcal{F}^{\flat}:(\mathbb{Y},\mathsf{D})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
such that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\mathbb{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}}
\scriptstyle{\exists!~\mathcal{F}^{\flat}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]}

is defined on objects as
\mathcal{F}^{\flat}(Y)=\mathcal{F}(Y)
and on maps as
\mathcal{F}^{\flat}(f)=\sum\limits^{\infty}_{n=0}\mathcal{F}\left(\partial^{n}[f]\right)^{\natural}_{n}
.

Example 7.7

Here are the differential constant homogenous maps and infinite sum decomposition in our examples of cofree Cartesian
k
-differential categories.

(i)

For a Cartesian left
k
-linear category
\mathbb{A}
, in its Faà di Bruno construction, a Faà di Bruno sequence
f^{\bullet}
is differential constant homogenous of degree
n
if
f^{m}=0
for all
m\neq n
, so
f^{\bullet}=(0,0,\ldots,0,f^{n},0,0,\ldots)
for some map
f^{n}
in
\mathbb{A}
which is
k
-multilinear and symmetric in its last
n
-arguments. For a Faà di Bruno sequence
f^{\bullet}
,
f^{\bullet}_{(n)}=(0,0,\ldots,0,f^{n},0,0,\ldots)
, and therefore
f^{\bullet}=(f_{0},0,\ldots)+(0,f_{1},0\ldots)+(0,0,f_{2},0,\ldots)+\ldots
as was desired.

(ii)

For a
k
-linear category
\mathbb{B}
with finite biproducts, in
\mathbb{B}^{\Delta}
there are no
\mathsf{D}^{\Delta}
-constant homogenous map of degree
n\geq 2
other than
(0,0)
. Those of degree
0
are of the form
(f,0)
and those of degree
1
are of the form
(0,g)
, for any maps
f
and
g
in
\mathbb{B}
. Then for an arbitrary map
(f,g)
in
\mathbb{B}^{\Delta}
,
(f,g)_{(0)}=(f,0)
and
(f,g)_{(1)}=(0,g)
.

(iii)

In
k\text{-}\mathsf{MOD}^{\mathcal{Q}}
, a
k
-linear morphism
f:\mathcal{Q}(M)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}M
is differential constant homogenous of degree
0
if for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
f\left(|x_{1},\ldots,x_{m+1}\rangle_{x_{0}}\right)=0
, and is of degree
n+1
if
f\left(|1\rangle_{(x_{0})}\right)=0
and
f\left(|x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}\rangle_{x_{0}}\right)=0
for all
m\neq n+1
, and of degree. Then for a
k
-linear morphism
f:\mathcal{Q}(M)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}M
,
f_{(0)}
is defined on pure symmetrized tensors as
f_{(0)}\left(|1\rangle_{(x_{0})}\right)=0
and
f_{(0)}\left(|x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}\rangle_{x_{0}}\right)=0
, while
f_{(n+1)}
is defined on pure symmetrized tensors as
f_{(n+1)}\left(|1\rangle_{(x_{0})}\right)=0
and
f_{(n+1)}\left(|x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}\rangle_{x_{0}}\right)=0
if
m\neq n
, and
f_{(n+1)}\left(|x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle_{x_{0}}\right)=f\left(|x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rangle_{x_{0}}\right)
.

8
Characterisation as Monad Algebras

In this section, we characterise cofree Cartesian
k
-differential categories this time as algebras of a monad on the category of Cartesian
k
-differential categories. This monad arises from the Faà di Bruno adjunction
[
14
, Corollary 3.13]
, which is the adjunction between the category of Cartesian
k
-differential categories and the category of Cartesian left
k
-linear categories where the left adjoint is the forgetful functor and the right adjoint is given by the Faà di Bruno construction. The resulting comonad was first studied by Cockett and Seely in
[
10
, Theorem 2.2.2]
where they also showed that the coalgebras of this comonad were precisely Cartesian
k
-differential categories
[
10
, Theorem 3.2.4]
, which we review below. In other words, the Faà di Bruno adjunction is comonadic. In this section, we will also explain why the Faà di Bruno adjunction is also monadic, that is, why the algebras of the induced monad correspond precisely to Cartesian
k
-left linear categories. As such, the Faà di Bruno comonad is of effective descent type
[
1
, Section 3]
.

Remark 8.1

We note that this entire section can be reformulated in terms of the
\mathcal{D}
-sequence construction
[
16
]
, since it also provides a right adjoint to the forgetful functor. Therefore, cofree Cartesian
k
-differential categories can also be characterised as algebras of the monad induced by the
\mathcal{D}
-sequence construction.

Let
\mathbb{CLLC}_{k}
be the category whose objects are Cartesian left
k
-linear categories and whose maps are Cartesian
k
-linear functors between them. Similarly, let
\mathbb{CDC}_{k}
be the category whose objects are Cartesian
k
-differential categories and whose maps are Cartesian
k
-differential functors between them. Let
\mathfrak{U}:\mathbb{CDC}_{k}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{CLLC}_{k}
be the forgetful functor which sends a Cartesian
k
-differential category to its underlying Cartesian
k
-linear,
\mathfrak{U}(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})=\mathbb{X}
, and sends a Cartesian
k
-differential functor to itself viewed as a Cartesian
k
-linear functor,
\mathfrak{U}(\mathcal{F})=\mathcal{F}
. Define the Faà di Bruno functor
\mathfrak{F}:\mathbb{CLLC}_{k}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{CDC}_{k}
which sends a Cartesian left
k
-linear category to its Faà di Bruno construction,
\mathfrak{F}(A)=\left(\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{A}],\mathsf{D}\right)
, and sends a Cartesian
k
-linear functor
\mathcal{A}:\mathbb{A}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{A}^{\prime}
to the induced unique Cartesian
k
-differential functor
{\mathsf{F}[\mathcal{A}]:\left(\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{A}],\mathsf{D}\right)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\left(\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{A}^{\prime}],\mathsf{D}\right)}
which makes the following diagram commute:

\textstyle{\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{A}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\exists!~\mathsf{F}[\mathcal{A}]}
\textstyle{\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{A}^{\prime}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{A}^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{A}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}^{\prime}}

so
\mathfrak{F}(\mathcal{A})=\mathsf{F}[\mathcal{A}]
. Explicitly, on objects
\mathsf{F}[\mathcal{A}](A)=A
and on maps
\mathsf{F}[\mathcal{A}](f^{\bullet})=\mathcal{A}(f^{\bullet})
.

The Faà di Bruno functor
\mathfrak{F}
is a right adjoint of the forgetful functor
\mathfrak{U}

[
14
, Corollary 3.13]
, so
\mathfrak{U}\dashv\mathfrak{F}
, which we call the
Faà di Bruno adjunction
. For a Cartesian left
k
-linear category
\mathbb{A}
, the counit of the Faà di Bruno adjunction is precisely
{\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{A}}:\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{A}]\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{A}}
. On the other hand, for a Cartesian
k
-differential category
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
, the unit of the Faà di Bruno adjunction is the induced unique Cartesian
k
-differential functor
\mathcal{N}_{(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})}:(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\left(\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{X}],\mathsf{D}\right)
that makes the following diagram commute:

\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\exists!~\mathcal{N}_{(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{X}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{X}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}}

Explicitly, on objects
\mathcal{N}_{(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})}(X)=X
, while on maps
\mathcal{N}_{(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})}(f)^{\bullet}=\partial^{\bullet}[f]=(\partial^{n}[f])_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
(as defined in Lemma
3.8
).

The Faà di Bruno adjunction induces a comonad
\underline{\mathfrak{F}}=\mathfrak{U}\circ\mathfrak{F}
on
\mathbb{CLLC}_{k}

[
10
, Theorem 2.2.2]
whose coalgebras correspond precisely to Cartesian
k
-differential categories
[
10
, Theorem 3.2.4]
. Explicitly, the comonad functor
{\underline{\mathfrak{F}}:\mathbb{CLLC}_{k}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{CLLC}_{k}}
is defined on objects and maps as
\underline{\mathfrak{F}}(-)=\mathsf{F}[-]
, and for a Cartesian left
k
-linear category
\mathbb{A}
, the comonad counit is
{\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{A}}:\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{A}]\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{A}}
, while the comonad comultiplication
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{A}}:\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{A}]\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{F}\left[\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{A}]\right]
is
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{A}}=\mathcal{N}_{(\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{A}],\mathsf{D})}
. An
\underline{\mathfrak{F}}
-coalgebra, which we can also be called a
Faà di Bruno coalgebra
, is a pair
\left(\mathbb{X},\mathcal{W}\right)
consisting of an Cartesian left
k
-linear category
\mathbb{X}
and a Cartesian
k
-linear functor
\mathcal{W}:\mathbb{X}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{X}]
such that the following diagrams commute:

\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{X}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{X}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{X}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{N}_{(\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{A}],\mathsf{D})}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{X}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{F}[\mathcal{W}]}
\textstyle{\mathsf{F}\left[\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{X}]\right]}

An
\underline{\mathfrak{F}}
-coalgebra morphism
\mathcal{F}:\left(\mathbb{X},\mathcal{W}\right)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\left(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathcal{W}^{\prime}\right)
between
\underline{\mathfrak{F}}
-coalgebras
\left(\mathbb{X},\mathcal{W}\right)
and
\left(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathcal{W}^{\prime}\right)
is a Cartesian
k
-linear functor
\mathcal{F}:\mathbb{X}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
such that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{W}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{X}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{F}[\mathcal{F}]}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{W}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{X}^{\prime}]}

Let
\mathbb{COALG}(\underline{\mathfrak{F}})
be the coEilenberg-Moore category of the comonad
\underline{\mathfrak{F}}
, or in other words, the category of
\underline{\mathfrak{F}}
-coalgebras and
\underline{\mathfrak{F}}
-coalgebra morphisms. Let
\mathfrak{K}:\mathbb{CDC}_{k}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{COALG}(\underline{\mathfrak{F}})
be the comparision functor of the Faà di Bruno adjunction
[
1
, Section 2.3]
, which tells us that every Cartesian
k
-differential category gives a
\underline{\mathfrak{F}}
-coalgebra. Indeed, if
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
is a Cartesian
k
-differential category, then
\mathfrak{K}(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}):=(\mathbb{X},\mathcal{N}_{(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})})
is a
\underline{\mathfrak{F}}
-coalgebra, and if
\mathcal{F}:\left(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}\right)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\left(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathsf{D}\right)
is a Cartesian
k
-differential functor, then
\mathfrak{K}(\mathcal{F}):=\mathcal{F}:\left(\mathbb{X},\mathcal{N}_{(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})}\right)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\left(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathcal{N}_{(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathsf{D})}\right)
is an
\underline{\mathfrak{F}}
-coalgebra morphism. It turns out that the converse is also true, that is, every
\underline{\mathfrak{F}}
-coalgebra is also a Cartesian
k
-differential category. This implies that the comparison functor is, in fact, an isomorphism with inverse
\mathfrak{K}^{-1}:\mathbb{COALG}(\underline{\mathfrak{F}})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{CDC}_{k}
. Indeed, let
\left(\mathbb{X},\mathcal{W}\right)
be a
\underline{\mathfrak{F}}
-coalgebra. Then for every map
f
of
\mathbb{X}
, its image is an
\mathbb{X}
-Faà di Bruno sequence
\mathcal{W}(f)^{\bullet}
. Then
\mathfrak{K}^{-1}(\mathbb{X},\mathcal{W}):=(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}^{\mathcal{W}})
is a Cartesian
k
-differential category where the differential combinator is defined as taking the second term of the Faà di Bruno sequence,
\mathsf{D}^{\mathcal{W}}[f]=\mathcal{W}(f)^{1}
, and if
\mathcal{F}:\left(\mathbb{X},\mathcal{W}\right)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\left(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathcal{W}^{\prime}\right)
is a
\underline{\mathfrak{F}}
-coalgebra morphism, then
\mathfrak{K}^{-1}(\mathcal{F}):=\mathcal{F}:\left(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}^{\mathcal{W}}\right)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\left(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathsf{D}^{\mathcal{W}^{\prime}}\right)
is a Cartesian
k
-differential functor. In summary:

Proposition 8.2

[
14
, Corollary 3.13]
The Faà di Bruno adjunction
\mathfrak{U}\dashv\mathfrak{F}
is comonadic, so
\mathbb{COALG}(\underline{\mathfrak{F}})\cong\mathbb{CDC}_{k}
.

The Faà di Bruno adjunction also induces a monad
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}=\mathfrak{F}\circ\mathfrak{U}
on
\mathbb{CDC}_{k}
, and the goal of this section is to show that the algebras of this monad are precisely the cofree Cartesian
k
-differential categories. This also implies that the algebras of this monad correspond precisely to Cartesian left
k
-linear categories. The monad functor
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}:\mathbb{CDC}_{k}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{CDC}_{k}
is defined on objects as
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}):=\left(\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{X}],\mathsf{D}\right)
, completely forgetting about the differential combinator of the input, and on maps as
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}(\mathcal{F})=\mathsf{F}[\mathcal{F}]
. For a Cartesian
k
-differential category
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
, the monad unit is
{\mathcal{N}_{(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})}:(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\left(\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{X}],\mathsf{D}\right)}
, while the monad multiplication
\mathcal{M}_{(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})}:\left(\mathsf{F}\left[\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{X}]\right],\mathsf{D}\right)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\left(\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{X}],\mathsf{D}\right)
is defined as
\mathcal{M}_{(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})}=\mathsf{F}[\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{X}}]
, that is, the unique Cartesian
k
-differential functor which makes the following diagram commute:

\textstyle{\mathsf{F}\left[\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{X}]\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\exists!~\mathcal{M}_{(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})}}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{X}]}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{X}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{X}}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{X}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{X}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}}

Explicitly, on objects
\mathcal{M}_{(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})}(X)=X
, while for maps, which is a Faà di Bruno sequence of Faà di Bruno sequences, it picks out the first term of each sequence,
\mathcal{M}_{(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})}\left(\left(f^{\bullet,n}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\right)=(f^{0,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. Let us now describe the algebras of this monad.

Definition 8.3

An
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}
-algebra
is a pair
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{X}\right)
consisting of a Cartesian
k
-differential category
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
and a Cartesian
k
-differential functor
\mathcal{X}:\left(\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{X}],\mathsf{D}\right)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\left(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}\right)
, called the
Faà di Bruno algebra structure
, and such that the following diagrams commute:

\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{N}_{(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{X}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{X}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{F}\left[\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{X}]\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{M}_{(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})}}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{F}[\mathcal{X}]}
\textstyle{\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{X}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{X}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{X}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{X}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}}

An
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}
-algebra morphism

\mathcal{F}:\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{X}\right)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\left((\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\right)
between
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}
-algebras
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{X}\right)
and
\left((\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\right)
is a Cartesian
k
-differential functor
\mathcal{F}:(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathsf{D})
such that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\mathsf{F}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{F}[\mathcal{F}]}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{X}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{X}^{\prime}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}^{\prime}}

Let
\mathbb{ALG}(\overline{\mathfrak{F}})
the Eilenberg-Moore category of the monad
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}
, that is, the category of
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}
-algebras and
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}
-algebra morphisms between them. We say that a Cartesian
k
-differential category
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
is a
Faà di Bruno algebra
if there exists a Cartesian
k
-differential functor
\mathcal{X}:\left(\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{X}],\mathsf{D}\right)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\left(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}\right)
such that
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{X}\right)
is an
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}
-algebra.

Later in Corollary
8.8
we will in fact show that Faà di Bruno algebra structure is unique. As such, being a Faà di Bruno algebra is a property of a Cartesian
k
-differential category. For now, let us unpack the definition of a Faà di Bruno algebra a bit more. So let
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{X}\right)
be an
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}
-algebra. Then
\mathcal{X}\circ\mathcal{N}_{(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})}=1_{\mathbb{X}}
implies that on objects
\mathcal{X}(X)
and for any map
f
on
\mathbb{X}
that
\mathcal{X}(f,\partial^{1}[f],\partial^{2}[f],\ldots)=f
. The other diagram says that for a Faà di Bruno sequence of Faà di Bruno sequences that
\mathcal{X}\left(\left(\mathcal{X}\left(f^{\bullet,n}\right)\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\right)=\mathcal{X}\left((f^{0,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\right)
. While a
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}
-algebra morphisms satisfies that
\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{X}(f^{\bullet}))=\mathcal{X}^{\prime}(\mathcal{F}(f^{\bullet}))
. By the adjunction, we also have the comparison functor
\mathfrak{C}:\mathbb{CLLC}_{k}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{ALG}(\overline{\mathfrak{F}})

[
1
, Section 2.3]
, which is defined on objects as
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{A}):=\left(\left(\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{A}],\mathsf{D}\right),\mathsf{F}[\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{A}}]\right)
and maps as
\mathfrak{C}(\mathcal{A})=\mathsf{F}[\mathcal{F}]
.

Lemma 8.4

For every Cartesian left
k
-linear category
\mathbb{A}
,
\left(\left(\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{A}],\mathsf{D}\right),\mathsf{F}[\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{A}}]\right)
is an
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}
-algebra.

Our objective to show the comparison functor is in fact an equivalence of categories. In order to show that every Faà di Bruno algebra is also a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category, by Theorem
6.13
, we must first explain why a Faà di Bruno algebra has a differential constant unit.

Lemma 8.5

Let
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{X}\right)
be an
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}
-algebra. Then
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
has a
\mathsf{D}
-constant unit
\varsigma
defined as
\varsigma_{X}=\mathcal{X}(1_{X},0,0,\ldots)
, and we also have that:

(i)

The following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{X}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{X}}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{X}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]}

so in particular, for every
\mathbb{X}
-Faà di Bruno sequence
f^{\bullet}:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
,
\varsigma_{Y}\circ\mathcal{X}\left(f^{\bullet}\right)=\varsigma_{Y}\circ f^{0}
.

(ii)

For every map
f:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
in
\mathbb{X}
,
(\varsigma_{Y}\circ\partial^{n}[f])_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a
\mathbb{X}
-Faà di Bruno sequence and
\mathcal{X}\left((\varsigma_{Y}\circ\partial^{n}[f])_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\right)=f
.

Furthermore, we also have that if
\mathcal{F}:\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{X}\right)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\left((\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\right)
is a
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}
-algebra morphism, then the functor
{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathcal{F}\right]:\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\right]}
, defined on objects and maps as
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathcal{F}\right](-)=\mathcal{F}(-)
, is a Cartesian
k
-linear functor and the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathcal{F}\right]}
\textstyle{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\right]}

Proof:
As explained in Example
6.10
.(
i
),
\varsigma^{\bullet}_{X}:=(1_{X},0,0,\ldots)
is a
\mathsf{D}
-constant unit in
\left(\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{X}],\mathsf{D}\right)
, and so in particular is a
\mathsf{D}
-constant and vanishes on
\mathsf{D}
-constants. Since
\mathcal{X}
is a Cartesian
k
-differential functor, by Lemma
6.3
,
\varsigma_{X}=\mathcal{X}(\varsigma^{\bullet}_{X})
is also a
\mathsf{D}
-constant. If
f:X\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}Y
is a
\mathsf{D}
-constant in
\mathbb{X}
, then since
\mathcal{N}_{(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})}
is also a Cartesian
k
-differential functor, we have that
\partial^{\bullet}[f]
is also a
\mathsf{D}
-constant in
\left(\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{X}],\mathsf{D}\right)
. So we compute that:

f\circ\varsigma_{X}=\mathcal{X}\left(\partial^{\bullet}[f]\right)\circ\mathcal{X}(\varsigma^{\bullet}_{X})=\mathcal{X}\left(\partial^{\bullet}[f]\circ\varsigma^{\bullet}_{X}\right)=\mathcal{X}\left(\partial^{\bullet}[f]\right)=f

and similarly that
\varsigma_{Y}\circ f=f
. So
\varsigma
vanishes on
\mathsf{D}
-constants in
\mathbb{X}
, and so we conclude that
\varsigma
is a
\mathsf{D}
-constant unit for
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
. For (
i
), observe that in
\left(\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{X}],\mathsf{D}\right)
, by Proposition
6.8
, that
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{X}}(\varsigma^{\bullet}\circ f^{\bullet})=f^{0}=\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{X}}(\varsigma^{\bullet}\circ\partial^{\bullet}[f^{0}])
. By Lemma
6.3
.(
ii
),
\varsigma^{\bullet}\circ f^{\bullet}
and
\varsigma^{\bullet}\circ\partial^{\bullet}[f^{0}]
are both
\mathsf{D}
-constants, so by Lemma
6.7
, since
\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{X}}(\varsigma^{\bullet}\circ f^{\bullet})=\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{X}}(\varsigma^{\bullet}\circ\partial^{\bullet}[f^{0}])
, then
\varsigma^{\bullet}\circ f^{\bullet}=\varsigma^{\bullet}\circ\partial^{\bullet}[f^{0}]
. Applying
\mathcal{X}
to this equality, we then obtain
\varsigma\circ\mathcal{X}\left(f^{\bullet}\right)=\varsigma\circ f^{0}
, and the desired diagram commutes by definition. For (
ii
), by Lemma
6.5
,
\varsigma
is
k
-linear so
(\varsigma\circ\partial^{n}[f])_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is indeed an
\mathbb{X}
-Faà di Bruno sequence. Then using (
i
), we compute that:

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}\left((\varsigma\circ\partial^{n}[f])_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\right)=\mathcal{X}\left(\left(\varsigma\circ\mathcal{X}\left(\partial^{\bullet}\left[\partial^{n}f\right]\right)\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\right)=\mathcal{X}\left(\left(\mathcal{X}\left(\varsigma^{\bullet}\circ\partial^{\bullet}\left[\partial^{n}f\right]\right)\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\right)=\mathcal{X}\left(\left(\partial^{n}[f]\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\right)=f

Then
\mathcal{X}\left((\varsigma\circ\partial^{n}[f])_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\right)=f
as desired. Now suppose that
\mathcal{F}:\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{X}\right)\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\left((\mathbb{X}^{\prime},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\right)
is a
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}
-algebra morphism. Since
\mathcal{F}
is Cartesian
k
-differential functor, by Lemma
6.3
it preserves
\mathsf{D}
-constants and so
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathcal{F}\right]
is well-defined. Clearly,
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathcal{F}\right]
preserves composition, and so it remains to show it also preserves identities. To do so, we must show that
\mathcal{F}
preserves the differential constant unit. Now since
\mathcal{F}
preserves zeros and identities, we have that
\mathcal{F}(\varsigma^{\bullet}_{X})=\varsigma^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{F}(X)}
. Therefore, we have that:

\mathcal{F}(\varsigma_{X})=\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{X}(\varsigma^{\bullet}_{X}))=\mathcal{X}(\mathcal{F}(\varsigma^{\bullet}_{X}))=\mathcal{X}^{\prime}(\varsigma^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{F}(X)})=\varsigma_{\mathcal{F}(X)}

So
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathcal{F}\right]
preserves identities and so is a functor. Since
\mathcal{F}
is Cartesian
k
-linear then so is
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathcal{F}\right]
.

\Box

We may now prove the main result of this section.

Theorem 8.6

A Cartesian
k
-differential category is cofree if and only if it is a Faà di Bruno algebra.

Proof:
For the
\Rightarrow
direction, let
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
be a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category, which by Theorem
6.13
, implies
\mathbb{X}
has a differential constant unit
\varsigma
such that
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}\right)
is a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category over
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]
. Then, using the couniversal property of
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}\right)
, define
\mathcal{X}:(\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{X}],\mathsf{D})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
as the unique Cartesian
k
-differential functor which makes the following diagram commute:

\textstyle{\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{X}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\exists!~\mathcal{X}}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{X}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]}

That
\mathcal{X}
is a Faà di Bruno algebra structure follows from the fact the diagrams on the left commute, which by Lemma
3.3
implies that the diagrams on the right commute.

0.0pt\raise-23.99998pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 76.90544pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{X}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 88.59218pt\raise-16.08124pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.1525pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{X}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 88.59218pt\raise-24.6pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-11.68674pt\raise-31.4375pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{X}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 41.08215pt\raise-36.59pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.1525pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{X}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 82.12416pt\raise-31.4375pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 82.12416pt\raise-31.4375pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\end{array}

So we conclude that
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{X}\right)
is a Faà di Bruno algebra.

For the
\Leftarrow
direction, let
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{X}\right)
be a Faà di Bruno algebra. By Lemma
8.5
,
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
has a differential constant unit
\varsigma
. Then, by Theorem
6.13
, to prove that
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
is cofree, it suffices to prove that the induced Cartesian
k
-differential functor
\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}:(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(\mathsf{F}\left[\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\right],\mathsf{D})
is an isomorphism. As before, to prove that
\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}
is an isomorphism, it is sufficient to prove that it is bijective on objects and maps, which allows us to avoid working with composition in the Faà di Bruno construction. Recall that
\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}
was worked out explicitly in Theorem
6.13
. Clearly,
\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}
is bijective on objects. On maps, we will explain why
\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}
is injective and surjective. Starting with injectivity, suppose that
\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}(f)=\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}(g)
. This implies that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\varsigma\circ\partial^{n}[f]=\varsigma\circ\partial^{n}[g]
, so we have an equality of
\mathbb{X}
-Faà di Bruno sequences
\left(\varsigma\circ\partial^{n}[f]\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}=\left(\varsigma\circ\partial^{n}[g]\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. Then by Lemma
8.5
.(
ii
), we have that:

f=\mathcal{X}\left(\left(\varsigma\circ\partial^{n}[f]\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\right)=\mathcal{X}\left(\left(\varsigma\circ\partial^{n}[g]\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\right)=g

So
f=g
, and so
\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}
is injective on maps. For surjectivity, let
f^{\bullet}
be a
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]
-Faà di Bruno sequence. This means that
f^{\bullet}
is also a
\mathbb{X}
-Faà di Bruno sequence and so
\mathcal{X}\left(f^{\bullet}\right)
is a map in
\mathbb{X}
. Then by Lemma
8.5
.(
i
), that
\partial^{n}[f^{\bullet}]^{0}=f^{n}
, and that
\varsigma
vanishes on
\mathsf{D}
-constants, we have that:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}\left(\mathcal{X}\left(f^{\bullet}\right)\right)=\left(\varsigma\circ\partial^{n}\left[\mathcal{X}\left(f^{\bullet}\right)\right]\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}=\left(\varsigma\circ\mathcal{X}\left(\partial^{n}\left[f^{\bullet}\right]\right)\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}=\left(\varsigma\circ\partial^{n}\left[f^{\bullet}\right]^{0}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}=(\varsigma\circ f^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}=(f^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

So
\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}\left(\mathcal{X}\left((f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\right)\right)=(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, thus
\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}
is surjective on maps. Therefore,
\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}
is bijective on maps, and we conclude that
\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}
is an isomorphism. Explicitly, the inverse
{\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}}^{-1}:\mathsf{F}\left[\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\right]\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{X}
is defined on objects as
{\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}}^{-1}(X)=X
and on maps as
{\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}^{\flat}}^{-1}\left((f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\right)=\mathcal{X}\left((f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\right)
. Therefore, we conclude that
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
is cofree.

\Box

We may explicitly write down the couniversal property for Faà di Bruno algebras and explain how to use the Faà di Bruno algebra structure to construct the induced unique Cartesian
k
-differential functors.

Corollary 8.7

Let
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{X}\right)
be a Faà di Bruno algebra. Then
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
has a differential constant unit
\varsigma
and
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}\right)
is a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category over
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]
, where in particular for any Cartesian
k
-differential category
(\mathbb{Y},\mathsf{D})
and Cartesian
k
-linear functor
{\mathcal{F}:\mathbb{Y}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{A}}
, the unique Cartesian
k
-differential functor
\mathcal{F}^{\flat}:(\mathbb{Y},\mathsf{D})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
such that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\mathbb{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}}
\scriptstyle{\exists!~\mathcal{F}^{\flat}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]}

is defined on objects as
\mathcal{F}^{\flat}(Y)=\mathcal{F}(Y)
and on maps as
\mathcal{F}^{\flat}(f)=\mathcal{X}\left(\mathcal{F}\left(\partial^{\bullet}[f]\right)\right)
.

We now may also explain why a Cartesian
k
-differential has at most one possible Faà di Bruno algebra structure.

Corollary 8.8

Faà di Bruno algebra structure on a Cartesian
k
-differential category, if it exists, is unique.

Proof:
Let
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
be a Cartesian
k
-differential category with Faà di Bruno algebra structures
{\mathcal{X}:\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{X}]\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{X}}
and
{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}:\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{X}]\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{X}}
. By Lemma
8.5
,
(\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D})
has a
\mathsf{D}
-constant unit
\varsigma
. By Lemma
6.5
, since
\mathsf{D}
-constant units are unique, we have that
\mathcal{X}(\varsigma^{\bullet})=\varsigma=\mathcal{X}^{\prime}(\varsigma^{\bullet})
. By Corollary
8.7
,
\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}\right)
is a cofree Cartesian
k
-differential category over
\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]
. By Lemma
8.5
.(
i
), we have that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{X}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{X}}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}_{\mathbb{X}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}_{\varsigma}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]}

and since
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
are Cartesian
k
-differential functors, by Lemma
3.3
, it follows that
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
.

\Box

Using Lemma
8.5
, we may explain why the Faà di Bruno adjunction is monadic. Consider again the comparison functor
\mathfrak{C}:\mathbb{CLLC}_{k}\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{ALG}(\overline{\mathfrak{F}})
. Then define the functor
\mathfrak{D}:\mathbb{ALG}(\overline{\mathfrak{F}})\allowbreak\mathrel{\mathop{\hbox to11.09988pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits}\mathbb{CLLC}_{k}
which sends a
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}
-algebra to its category of differential constants, so
\mathfrak{D}\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{X}\right)=\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]
, and is defined on maps as
\mathfrak{D}(\mathcal{F})=\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathcal{F}\right]
, which is well-defined by Lemma
8.5
. By Lemma
6.12
, we have that
\mathfrak{D}\left(\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{A})\right)=\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathsf{F}[\mathbb{A}]\right]\cong\mathbb{A}
, and it follows from Theorem
8.6
and Lemma
3.5
that
\mathfrak{C}\left(\mathfrak{D}\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{X}\right)\right)=\left(\left(\mathsf{F}\left[\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]\right],\mathsf{D}\right),\mathsf{F}[\mathcal{E}_{\mathsf{D}\text{-}\mathsf{con}\left[\mathbb{X}\right]}]\right)\cong\left((\mathbb{X},\mathsf{D}),\mathcal{X}\right)
. Therefore,
\mathbb{ALG}(\overline{\mathfrak{F}})
and
\mathbb{CLLC}_{k}
are equivalent via the Faà di Bruno adjunction’s comparison functor. So, we conclude with the following statement:

Proposition 8.9

The Faà di Bruno adjunction
\mathfrak{U}\dashv\mathfrak{F}
is monadic, so
\mathbb{ALG}(\overline{\mathfrak{F}})\cong\mathbb{CLLC}_{k}
.
